Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect E from H.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: F, L, M, B, I.
    *   Node H is connected to: G, B, I.

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to H:**
    Any path from E to H must eventually use one of the edges connected to H as its final step. These edges are (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H). If we remove all three of these edges, node H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. This would be a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Check for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To confirm if 3 is the minimum, I will look for edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 'k' (by Menger's Theorem).

    Let's identify some paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - I - H
        Edges: {(E, I), (I, H)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}

    Let's check if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The edges in Path 1 are (E, B) and (B, H).
    *   The edges in Path 2 are (E, I) and (I, H).
    *   The edges in Path 3 are (E, F), (F, G), and (G, H).

    Comparing the sets of edges, there are no common edges between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these are three distinct edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.
    We have already identified a cut set of size 3: [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]. This set of edges, when removed, isolates H from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path exists between E and H.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges to remove is 3. The cut set [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)] achieves this by isolating node H.

The final answer is \boxed{Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]}
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']]}

============================================================
Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and H.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: M, L, F, B.
    *   Node H is connected to: B, G, I.

2.  **Consider the "bottleneck" nodes for H:** Any path from E to H must pass through one of H's direct neighbors: B, G, or I. Therefore, removing the edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H) would completely isolate H from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from E. This would be a cut of size 3.

3.  **Explore for a smaller cut (e.g., size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** It's clear that there are multiple independent paths between E and H. For example, E-B-H and E-F-X-G-H are two distinct paths. Removing a single edge cannot break all paths.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's try to find two edges that could disconnect E and H. If we remove any two edges directly connected to H, say (B, H) and (G, H), the path E-M-J-D-P-I-H (which uses (I, H)) would still exist. Similarly, removing any other pair of edges connected to H would leave at least one path. This suggests that a cut of size 2 might not be possible.

4.  **Verify the minimum cut using Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):**
    To confirm that 3 is the minimum, I will look for three edge-disjoint paths between E and H.
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - X - G - H
        Edges: (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - D - P - I - H
        Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, D), (D, P), (P, I), (I, H)

    These three paths are edge-disjoint because they start with distinct edges from E ((E, B), (E, F), (E, M)) and end with distinct edges into H ((B, H), (G, H), (I, H)). Since there are at least three edge-disjoint paths between E and H, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them is at least 3.

5.  **Confirm the cut set:**
    Since we have identified a cut set of size 3, [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)], and we have determined that at least 3 edges are required, this cut set is minimal. Removing these three edges ensures that H has no connections to any other node, thus disconnecting it from E.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']]}
Progress: 8/280 images processed.

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and D. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and D.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node F (black) is connected to X, K, G, E.
    *   Node D (black) is connected to J, P.

2.  **Analyze the connections of node D:**
    Node D has only two direct connections to the rest of the graph: (J, D) and (P, D). If these two edges are removed, node D will be completely isolated from all other nodes, including F. This would guarantee that no path exists between F and D.

3.  **Consider the number of edges for this cut:**
    Removing edges (J, D) and (P, D) requires removing 2 edges.

4.  **Check for alternative paths and minimum cut size:**
    To confirm if 2 is the minimum, I look for edge-disjoint paths between F and D. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes.
    Let's identify two edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** F - E - M - J - D
        Edges: (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, D)
    *   **Path 2:** F - G - H - I - P - D
        Edges: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   (F, E) is distinct from (F, G).
    *   (E, M) is distinct from (G, H).
    *   (M, J) is distinct from (H, I).
    *   (J, D) is distinct from (I, P) and (P, D).
    *   The paths do not share any common edges.

    Since I have found two edge-disjoint paths between F and D, it means that at least 2 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We found a way to disconnect F and D by removing 2 edges: (J, D) and (P, D). Since we also established that a minimum of 2 edges must be removed (due to the existence of two edge-disjoint paths), this cut set is indeed minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node G is connected to: X, K, F, H.
    *   Node N is connected to: C, O, Y.

2.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   A simple approach is to isolate one of the target nodes by removing all its incident edges. For N, this would mean removing (N, C), (N, O), and (N, Y). This is a cut of size 3. For G, this would mean removing (G, X), (G, K), (G, F), and (G, H). This is a cut of size 4.
    *   We are looking for the *minimum* number of edges, so a cut of size 3 or 4 might not be minimal.

3.  **Look for bottlenecks or critical edges:**
    Let's analyze the structure of the graph around G. All paths originating from G must pass through one of its direct neighbors: X, K, F, or H.
    *   **Paths through H:** Any path from G that starts with G-H will use the edge (G, H).
    *   **Paths through F, K, or X:**
        *   If a path starts G-F, it must continue from F. To reach N, it must eventually connect to the "main" part of the graph that leads to N. Looking at F's neighbors (X, K, G, E), the only edge that leads away from the immediate G-cluster (G, X, K, F) towards the rest of the graph (and N) is (F, E).
        *   If a path starts G-K, it must go K-F (or K-X-F) and then F-E to continue towards N.
        *   If a path starts G-X, it must go X-F (or X-K-F) and then F-E to continue towards N.

4.  **Formulate a hypothesis for a minimal cut:**
    Based on the observation in step 3, it appears that all paths from G to N must either:
    a.  Pass through the edge (G, H).
    b.  Pass through the edge (F, E) (after potentially going through X or K to reach F).

    Therefore, if we remove both (G, H) and (F, E), we should disconnect G from N.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set: [(G, H), (F, E)]**
    *   **Path type 1 (via H):** Any path G -> H -> ... -> N is broken by the removal of (G, H).
    *   **Path type 2 (via F, K, or X):**
        *   G -> F -> E -> ... -> N: Broken by the removal of (F, E).
        *   G -> K -> F -> E -> ... -> N: Broken by the removal of (F, E).
        *   G -> X -> F -> E -> ... -> N: Broken by the removal of (F, E).

    Since all possible paths from G to N fall into one of these categories, removing (G, H) and (F, E) successfully disconnects G from N. This cut set has 2 edges.

6.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible (i.e., a cut of size 1):**
    To have a cut of size 1, there must be a single "bridge" edge whose removal disconnects G and N. Let's try to find at least two edge-disjoint paths between G and N.
    *   Path 1: G - H - B - C - N
    *   Path 2: G - F - E - I - P - O - N
    These two paths do not share any edges. Since there are at least two edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size must be at least 2 (by Menger's Theorem).

7.  **Conclusion:**
    We found a cut set of size 2, and we've shown that a cut of size 1 is not possible. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, E)]

First, I will identify all distinct paths from E to I. To do this systematically, I will consider the direct neighbors of E (L, M, F, G, B) and the direct neighbors of I (J, H, C, O, P).

**Channel 1: Paths through M and J**
*   E - L - M - J - I
*   E - L - M - J - P - I
*   E - M - J - I

*   E - F - K - G - H - I

*   E - G - H - I
    *   All these paths pass through node H.

    *   This path passes through node H.

*   E - B - C - O - I
*   E - B - N - C - I
*   E - B - N - C - O - I
    *   All these paths pass through node C.

**Channel 5: Paths through B, N, Y, and O**
*   E - B - N - Y - O - I
    *   This path passes through node B and node O.

To disconnect E and I, I need to remove nodes such that all these channels are broken.

Let's try to find the minimum number of nodes. This is equivalent to finding the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between E and I (Menger's Theorem).

I will try to identify vertex-disjoint paths (excluding E and I):
1.  **Path 1: E - M - J - I**
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding E, I): {M, J}
2.  **Path 2: E - G - H - I**
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding E, I): {G, H}
3.  **Path 3: E - B - C - I**
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding E, I): {B, C}

Let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint:
*   Path 1 uses {M, J}.
*   Path 2 uses {G, H}.
*   Path 3 uses {B, C}.
These three sets of nodes are completely disjoint. Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and I is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3. I will choose nodes that effectively block these main channels.
Consider the nodes J, H, and B.

Let's verify if removing nodes [J, H, B] disconnects E from I:
*   **Removing J:** This breaks all paths in Channel 1 (E-L-M-J-I, E-L-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I).
*   **Removing H:** This breaks all paths in Channel 2 (E-F-X-K-G-H-I, E-F-K-G-H-I, E-F-G-H-I, E-G-H-I) and also the path E-B-H-I from Channel 3.
*   **Removing B:** This breaks all paths in Channel 4 (E-B-C-I, E-B-C-O-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-C-O-I) and Channel 5 (E-B-N-Y-O-I), as well as the path E-B-H-I (already covered by H).

After removing J, H, and B:
*   Any path from E through L or M would need to go through J to reach I, but J is removed.
*   Any path from E through F or G would need to go through H to reach I, but H is removed.
*   Any path from E through B is impossible because B is removed.

Therefore, removing nodes J, H, and B successfully disconnects E from I. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes that works, this is the minimum.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from H.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of N and H:**
    *   Neighbors of N: Y, O, C, B
    *   Neighbors of H: G, I, C, B

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths between N and H:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut separating two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths from N to H:
    *   **Path 1: N - C - H**
        Edges: {(N, C), (C, H)}
    *   **Path 2: N - B - H**
        Edges: {(N, B), (B, H)}
        (Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint as they use different intermediate nodes C and B, and different edges from N and to H).
    *   **Path 3: N - O - P - I - H**
        Edges: {(N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)}
        (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as it uses entirely different edges and intermediate nodes (O, P, I) that are not C or B).

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: (N, C), (C, H)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (N, B), (B, H)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)
    No edge is common to any two of these three paths. Therefore, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N from H is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    We need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects N from H. A straightforward approach is to cut all "outgoing" edges from N that lead towards H.
    *   Remove edge (N, C): This breaks Path 1 (N-C-H) and any other path starting N-C-....
    *   Remove edge (N, B): This breaks Path 2 (N-B-H) and any other path starting N-B-....
    *   Remove edge (N, O): This breaks Path 3 (N-O-P-I-H) and any other path starting N-O-....

    Let's verify if removing these three edges [(N, C), (N, B), (N, O)] completely disconnects N from H:
    *   After removing (N, C), N can no longer reach node C.
    *   After removing (N, B), N can no longer reach node B.
    *   After removing (N, O), N can no longer reach node O.
    Node N is initially connected to Y, O, C, B. After removing (N, O), (N, C), and (N, B), N is only connected to Y. Node Y is only connected to N and has no other connections to the rest of the graph, thus it cannot lead to H. Therefore, N is completely isolated from H.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, which implies a minimum cut of at least 3. We have also identified a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects N from H. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of edges to remove.

This cut set has a size of 2.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are H and N.
2.  **Identify Connections to Target Nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to G, I, and B.
    *   Node N is connected to C, O, and B.
3.  **Initial Strategy - Isolate a Node:** A straightforward way to disconnect two nodes is to remove all edges incident to one of them.
    *   To isolate N, we would remove the edges (N, C), (N, O), and (N, B). This would require removing 3 edges.
    *   To isolate H, we would remove the edges (H, G), (H, I), and (H, B). This would also require removing 3 edges.
    This suggests that the minimum number of edges to remove might be 3.
4.  **Verify Minimum Cut using Menger's Theorem (Edge-Disjoint Paths):** To confirm that 3 is indeed the minimum, we can try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and N. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut is at least *k*.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from H to N:
    *   **Path 1 (P1): H - B - N**
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}
    *   **Path 2 (P2): H - I - C - N**
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}
    *   **Path 3 (P3): H - G - F - E - B - C - O - N**
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, N)}

5.  **Check for Edge Disjointness:**
    *   Edges in P1: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Edges in P2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Edges in P3: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, N)
    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are distinct. No edge is shared between any two of these paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, Menger's Theorem states that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. As we also identified a cut set of 3 edges (e.g., [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]) that successfully disconnects H from N, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and J.

First, I identify all direct connections from F and to J:
*   F is connected to E, X, K.
*   J is connected to M, P, D.

Next, I categorize the paths from F to J based on the initial edges from F:

**Group 1: Paths starting with (F, E)**
These paths are:
1.  F - E - M - J
2.  F - E - L - M - J

All paths in this group can be cut by removing a single edge:
*   (F, E) - This cuts both paths at their origin from F.
*   (M, J) - This cuts both paths at their destination to J.
*   Alternatively, removing (E, M) and (L, M) would also cut these paths, but this would be 2 edges, which is not optimal if a single edge can do the job.

**Group 2: Paths starting with (F, X) or (F, K)**
Let's trace these paths:
*   From F, we can go to X or K.
*   From X, we can go to K or G (or F).
*   From K, we can go to X or G (or F).
*   Crucially, all paths from F that do not use E, must eventually pass through G.
    *   Example: F - X - G - ...
    *   Example: F - K - G - ...
    *   Example: F - X - K - G - ... (or F - K - X - G - ...)
*   From G, the only outgoing edge is (G, H).
*   From H, the only outgoing edge towards the main cluster of nodes (C, N, B, I, O, P, Y) is (H, B).

Therefore, all paths in Group 2 must pass through the sequence of edges (G, H) and (H, B).
To cut all paths in Group 2, we can remove a single edge:
*   (G, H) - This cuts all paths from F (via X or K) before they reach H and B.
*   (H, B) - This cuts all paths from F (via X or K) before they reach the central cluster of nodes.

**Finding the Minimum Cut Set:**
To completely disconnect F and J, we must cut at least one edge from Group 1 paths AND at least one edge from Group 2 paths.

Let's consider removing one edge from Group 1 and one edge from Group 2. This would result in a cut set of size 2.

**Candidate Cut Set 1: [(F, E), (G, H)]**
1.  Removing (F, E) breaks all paths in Group 1 (F-E-M-J and F-E-L-M-J). F can no longer reach E, L, M, and thus J via this branch.
2.  Removing (G, H) breaks all paths in Group 2. F can reach X and K, and from there, G. However, with (G, H) removed, F cannot proceed from G to H, and thus cannot reach B, C, N, Y, O, P, I, and ultimately J via this branch.
Since all paths from F to J fall into either Group 1 or Group 2, removing these two edges ensures complete disconnection.

This is a cut set of size 2. It is not possible to disconnect F and J with only one edge, as there are clearly at least two independent "bottleneck" regions (one via E, and one via G-H-B).

Other valid cut sets of size 2 include:
*   [(F, E), (H, B)]
*   [(M, J), (G, H)]
*   [(M, J), (H, B)]

The minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

The final answer is structured as requested.

6.  **Test for Two Node Removal:**
    *   Since removing E breaks all paths through L and E, and removing J breaks all paths through J, removing both E and J will sever all possible connections from M to H.
    *   If E is removed, M can no longer reach H via L or E.
    *   If J is removed, M can no longer reach H via J.
    *   Since all paths from M must start with L, E, or J, removing both E and J ensures no path remains.

    *   Group 2: Paths that go through J (e.g., M-J-I-..., M-J-P-...).

5.  To disconnect M and N, we must sever all paths in both groups.
    *   Removing node H will break all paths in Group 1.
    *   Removing node J will break all paths in Group 2.

6.  Therefore, removing both H and J will ensure that no path exists between M and N. This gives a cut set of size 2: {H, J}.

7.  Verify if a smaller cut set (size 1) is possible:
    *   If only H is removed, paths through J (e.g., M-J-I-C-N) still exist.
    *   If only J is removed, paths through H (e.g., M-E-H-B-N) still exist.
    *   If any other single node (e.g., E, I, P, B, C, O, L, D, F, G, K, X, Y) is removed, at least one path between M and N will remain. (This was checked in the thought process).

8.  Since no single node removal can disconnect M and N, and we found a set of 2 nodes that does, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, J]

*   Neighbors of G: F, H, K, X
*   Neighbors of N: B, C, Y, O

Next, I systematically explore all possible paths from G to N. I'll categorize paths based on the first edge taken from G.

**Paths starting from G-F:**
1.  G - F - E - B - N
2.  G - F - E - B - C - N
3.  G - F - E - B - O - N
4.  G - F - E - B - Y - N
5.  G - F - M - L - E - B - N (and its variations to C, O, Y via B)
6.  G - F - X - K - G (cycle, not a path to N)

**Paths starting from G-H:**
1.  G - H - B - N
2.  G - H - B - C - N
3.  G - H - B - O - N
4.  G - H - B - Y - N
5.  G - H - I - C - N
6.  G - H - I - J - P - O - N
7.  G - H - I - J - D - P - O - N

**Paths starting from G-K:**
1.  G - K - X - F - E - B - N (and its variations to C, O, Y via B)
2.  G - K - X - F - M - L - E - B - N (and its variations to C, O, Y via B)

**Paths starting from G-X:**
1.  G - X - F - E - B - N (and its variations to C, O, Y via B)
2.  G - X - F - M - L - E - B - N (and its variations to C, O, Y via B)

Now, I look for "bottleneck" edges that all these paths must traverse.
I observe that all paths from G eventually lead to one of two main "entry points" into the region connected to N:
1.  Through node B.
2.  Through node I (which then connects to C, J, P, D, and eventually O, N).

Let's analyze the edges that connect the "G-side" of the graph (nodes G, F, H, K, X, M, L, E) to the "N-side" (nodes N, B, C, Y, O, I, J, P, D).

*   All paths that go through F, K, or X eventually lead to E, and then to B. The critical edge here is **(E, B)**. If I remove (E, B), all paths like G-...-F-E-B-N, G-...-K-X-F-E-B-N, G-...-X-F-E-B-N, G-...-F-M-L-E-B-N are broken.

*   All paths that go through H can either go to B or to I.
    *   Paths like G-H-B-N are broken by removing **(H, B)**.
    *   Paths like G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N are broken by removing **(H, I)**.

Therefore, by removing the three edges **(E, B), (H, B), and (H, I)**, I can disconnect all identified paths from G to N.

Let's verify if this is the minimum number of edges:
*   If I remove only 2 edges, for example, (E, B) and (H, B), then paths like G-H-I-C-N would still exist.
*   If I remove only 2 edges, for example, (E, B) and (H, I), then paths like G-H-B-N would still exist.
*   If I remove only 2 edges, for example, (H, B) and (H, I), then paths like G-F-E-B-N would still exist.

Since removing any two edges leaves at least one path, a minimum of 3 edges is required. The identified set of 3 edges successfully disconnects G from N.

Final Answer:

    *   Let's examine how paths can leave this {G, X, K, F} subgraph to reach the rest of the graph (and eventually N).
        *   G has edges to E and H.
        *   F has an edge to E.
        *   X and K have no edges to any node outside of {G, X, K, F}.

3.  **Identify critical "exit" points from G's side:**
    Any path starting from G and aiming for N must eventually leave the {G, X, K, F} subgraph. The only edges that lead out of this subgraph are G-E, G-H, and F-E.
    *   If a path uses the G-E edge, it passes through node E.
    *   If a path uses the G-H edge, it passes through node H.
    *   If a path uses the F-E edge (meaning it went G -> X/K/F -> F -> E), it also passes through node E.

    Therefore, every single path from G to N *must* pass through either node E or node H.

4.  **Test for a single-node cut:**
    *   If I remove E, paths can still go through H (e.g., G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-O-N). So, removing E alone is not enough.

    *   Since no single node (E or H) can disconnect G from N, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Test for a two-node cut:**
    *   Based on the analysis in step 3, if all paths must pass through E or H, then removing both E and H should disconnect G from N.
    *   Let's verify: If E and H are removed, the nodes G, X, K, F are left connected only to each other. They have no remaining connections to any other part of the graph where N resides. Thus, G is completely disconnected from N.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the cut set is [E, H].

1.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**

    *   Node D is directly connected to J, P, O, Y.

2.  **Consider a simple cut:**

    The edges connected to D are: (J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D).
    Removing these 4 edges would completely disconnect D. This is a valid cut of size 4.

3.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (e.g., 3 edges):**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum, we must show that no set of 3 edges can disconnect F from D. Let's try to identify critical "bottleneck" edges.

    Consider the "entry points" to the D-side of the graph (nodes J, P, O, Y, which are directly connected to D). Any path from F to D must pass through one of these nodes. We need to cut all paths leading to these nodes.

    Let's try various combinations of 3 edges and check if any path remains:

    *   **Scenario A: Removing 3 direct edges to D.**
        If we remove any 3 of the 4 edges connected to D (e.g., (J,D), (P,D), (O,D)), the remaining edge (Y,D) would still allow a path to D (e.g., F-E-B-N-O-Y-D). This applies to any combination of 3 direct edges to D. Thus, removing 3 direct edges to D is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario B: Removing 3 "bridge" edges from the F-side to the D-side.**
        Let's identify edges that connect the "left" part of the graph (closer to F) to the "right" part (closer to D).
        Consider the set of nodes {F, K, X, G, E, M, L, H, B, C, N, I} as the "F-side" and {D, J, P, O, Y} as the "D-side".
        The edges connecting these two sets are:
        (M, J)
        (I, J)
        (I, P)
        (C, O)
        (N, O)
        There are 5 such edges. If we remove any 3 of these 5 edges, at least two paths will remain. For example:
        *   If we remove [(M,J), (I,J), (I,P)]: Paths through (C,O) and (N,O) to D would still exist (e.g., F-E-B-C-O-D, F-E-B-N-O-D).
        *   If we remove [(C,O), (N,O), (M,J)]: Paths through (I,J) and (I,P) to D would still exist (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-J-D, F-X-G-H-I-P-D).

    *   **Scenario C: Trying a mixed set of 3 edges.**
        Let's try to cut edges that are bottlenecks for different "channels" of communication.
        *   Consider cutting [(M,J), (I,P), (C,O)].
            *   (M,J) cuts paths like F-K-M-J-D.
            *   (I,P) cuts paths like F-X-G-H-I-P-D.
            *   (C,O) cuts paths like F-E-B-C-O-D.

        *   Consider cutting [(M,J), (I,J), (N,O)].

            *   (I,J) cuts paths via I to J.
            *   (N,O) cuts paths via N to O.

    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C, O, P}

*   Path 1: {M}
*   Path 2: {F, X, G, H, I}
*   Path 3: {B, C, O, P}

Comparing the sets of intermediate nodes:
*   {M} has no common nodes with {F, X, G, H, I}.
*   {M} has no common nodes with {B, C, O, P}.
*   {F, X, G, H, I} has no common nodes with {B, C, O, P}.

Since these three paths are node-disjoint, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E and J.

Now, let's find a cut set of size 3. We can achieve this by removing the immediate neighbors of E that lead to distinct branches, or the immediate neighbors of J that lead to distinct branches.

Consider the neighbors of E: M, L, F, B.
*   **M** is a direct link to J (E-M-J).
*   **F** leads to a branch (E-F-...).
*   **B** leads to another branch (E-B-...).
*   **L** leads to M (E-L-M-J).

1.  Removing **M** breaks the direct path E-M-J and also the path E-L-M-J.

After removing M, F, and B, all paths from E to J are severed. Since we have found a cut set of size 3, and we know the minimum is at least 3, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

        These paths must end at H via H's other neighbors: G or I.

        *   E - L - M - F - G - H

    *   Bundle 1: All paths that pass through node B.
    *   Bundle 2: All paths that pass through node F and node G.

    *   J - P - O - C - B - F
    *   J - P - O - Y - N - B - F

To find the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for a minimum vertex cut. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of vertices in a vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes. Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths (excluding J and F).

I can identify the following three vertex-disjoint paths:

*   **Path 1:** J - M - E - F (Nodes: M, E)
*   **Path 2:** J - I - H - X - F (Nodes: I, H, X)
*   **Path 3:** J - D - P - O - N - B - F (Nodes: D, P, O, N, B)

These three paths are vertex-disjoint because they do not share any intermediate nodes.
*   Path 1 uses {M, E}.
*   Path 2 uses {I, H, X}.
*   Path 3 uses {D, P, O, N, B}.
Since these sets of intermediate nodes are disjoint, these are three vertex-disjoint paths.

Because there are at least three vertex-disjoint paths between J and F, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

*   **B** is a critical node for many paths in Branch 2 (J - I - C - B - F) and all paths in Branch 3 (J - D/P - O - ... - B - F).
*   **H** is a critical node for many paths in Branch 2 (J - I - H - ... - F).

1.  **Remove M:** This immediately breaks all paths in Branch 1 (J - M - E - F and J - M - L - E - F).
2.  **Remove B:** This breaks all paths in Branch 3 (J - D/P - O - N/C/Y - B - F) and the path J - I - C - B - F from Branch 2.
3.  **Remove H:** This breaks the remaining paths in Branch 2 that go through H (J - I - H - X - F, J - I - H - G - X - F, J - I - H - G - K - F).

Let's systematically check all possible paths from J after removing M, B, and H:

*   **From J to M:** M is removed, so no paths through M.
*   **From J to I:** I is connected to C and H. H is removed. So, J can only go to I -> C.

    *   From O, the connections are to P, D, N, C, Y.

Image name: PG_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are O (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect O and H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node O is connected to: M, P, Y, N.
    *   Node H is connected to: I, C, X, G.

2.  **Analyze the structure of the graph to find potential "bottleneck" nodes or edges:**
    All paths from O to H must eventually pass through one of H's direct neighbors: I, C, X, or G.
    Let's examine the connections of G: G is connected to H and X. This means any path from O to G (and then to H) must first pass through X. Therefore, if we can cut all paths to I, C, and X, we will also implicitly cut all paths to G.

3.  **Consider a vertex cut:**
    If we remove the nodes {I, C, X}, let's see if O and H are disconnected.
    *   If I, C, and X are removed, H is only connected to G.
    *   G is connected to X. Since X is removed, G is now only connected to H.
    *   Can O reach G? All paths from O to G must pass through X (e.g., O-N-B-E-F-X-G, O-M-L-E-F-X-G). Since X is removed, O cannot reach G.
    *   Therefore, removing the nodes {I, C, X} successfully disconnects O from H. This is a vertex cut of size 3.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges in an s-t edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint s-t paths. Since we found a vertex cut of size 3, the minimum edge cut must also be 3.

5.  **Find a specific edge cut of size 3:**
    We need to select three edges such that their removal severs all paths from O to H. Based on the vertex cut {I, C, X}, we need to cut all paths leading into I, C, and X from the O-side of the graph.

    Let's consider the following edges:
    *   **(J, I):** This edge is crucial for paths leading to I.
        *   Paths like O-M-J-I-H and O-P-D-J-I-H would be cut.
    *   **(H, C):** This edge directly connects C to H, cutting all paths that reach H via C.
        *   Paths like O-N-B-C-H, O-P-Y-N-C-H, and O-M-L-E-B-C-H would be cut.
    *   **(F, X):** This edge is a bottleneck for paths leading to X (and consequently to G).
        *   Paths like O-N-B-E-F-X-H, O-N-B-E-F-K-X-H, O-M-L-E-F-X-H, O-M-L-E-F-K-X-H would be cut.
        *   Since G is connected to X, and F is a primary entry point to X from the O-side (via E), cutting (F,X) also effectively cuts paths to G (e.g., O-N-B-E-F-X-G-H).

6.  **Verify the chosen cut set: [(J, I), (H, C), (F, X)]**
    *   **Removing (J, I):** All paths from O that pass through J to I (and then to H) are broken. This covers all paths to H via I.
    *   **Removing (H, C):** All paths from O that pass through C to H are broken. This covers all paths to H via C.
    *   **Removing (F, X):** All paths from O that pass through F to X (and then to H or G) are broken. This covers all paths to H via X and G.

    Since all identified path categories (via I, C, X, and G) are severed by these three edges, O and H are completely disconnected.

7.  **Confirm minimality:**
    As established by the vertex cut of size 3, it is not possible to disconnect O and H with fewer than 3 edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, I), (H, C), (F, X)]

Therefore, a two-node removal is not sufficient.

**Attempt 3: Cut Set of Size 3**

    *   If a path goes through F, it must then pass through E to proceed towards N.
    *   Therefore, every path from G to N must pass through either node H or node E.

5.  **Test for a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If H is removed: Paths like G-F-E-I-C-N would still exist.
    *   If F is removed: Paths like G-H-B-N would still exist.
    *   If E is removed: Paths like G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-N would still exist.
    *   If I is removed: Paths like G-H-B-N, G-H-E-L-M-J-P-N would still exist.
    *   If B is removed: Paths like G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-P-N, G-H-E-L-M-J-P-N would still exist.
    *   If C is removed: Paths like G-H-B-N, G-H-I-P-N, G-H-E-L-M-J-P-N would still exist.
    *   If P is removed: Paths like G-H-B-N, G-H-B-C-N, G-H-I-C-N would still exist.
    *   No single node removal disconnects G and N.

6.  **Test for a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Based on step 4, since all paths from G to N must pass through H or E, removing both H and E should disconnect G from N.
    *   **Verification of removing H and E:**
        *   If H is removed, G is connected to F, K, X.
        *   If E is also removed, then F (which was connected to E) no longer has a path to the rest of the graph containing N.
        *   The nodes G, F, K, X form a connected component that is now isolated from all other nodes in the graph.
        *   Therefore, G is completely disconnected from N.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the set [H, E] achieves this disconnection.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

*   Edges in Path 3: {(M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B)}

    *   If we remove Y, Path 1 is broken.
    *   If we remove B, Paths 2, 3, and 4 are broken.
    *   If we remove E, Paths 5, 6, 7, and 8 are broken.

5.  Let's test if removing any two of these nodes is sufficient:
    *   If we remove Y and B: Paths 1, 2, 3, 4 are broken. However, Paths 5, 6, 7, 8 (which go through E) remain, so N is still connected to P.
    *   If we remove Y and E: Paths 1, 5, 6, 7, 8 are broken. However, Paths 2, 3, 4 (which go through B) remain, so N is still connected to P.
    *   If we remove B and E: Paths 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 are broken. However, Path 1 (which goes through Y) remains, so N is still connected to P.

6.  Since removing any two of {Y, B, E} leaves at least one path intact, we must remove all three nodes {Y, B, E} to disconnect N from P.
    *   Removing Y breaks Path 1.
    *   Removing B breaks Paths 2, 3, 4.
    *   Removing E breaks Paths 5, 6, 7, 8.
    *   With Y, B, and E removed, N has no outgoing connections to any other white node, thus it is completely disconnected from P.

7.  This forms a cut set of size 3: [Y, B, E].

8.  Alternatively, consider the nodes directly connected to P: O, I, and J (via D).
    *   All paths from N to P must end by going through one of these three nodes.
    *   If we remove O, Paths 1 and 2 are broken.
    *   If we remove I, Paths 3, 4, 7, and 8 are broken.
    *   If we remove J, Paths 5 and 6 are broken.
    *   Similar to the previous analysis, removing any two of {O, I, J} would leave at least one path group intact. Therefore, removing all three nodes {O, I, J} would also disconnect N from P. This is another cut set of size 3.

9.  Since we have found a cut set of size 3, and we have systematically shown that no cut set of size 1 or 2 is sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [Y, B, E]

    *   M - L - E - B (Path through L then E)
    *   M - K - F - E - B (Path through K, F then E)
    *   M - E - C - B (Path through E, C)
    *   M - E - C - N - B (Path through E, C, N)
    All paths in this category pass through node E. Therefore, removing E would break all these paths.

    **Category 2: Paths starting M -> K (and not using E)**
    Since we are considering paths *not* using E, these paths must continue from K.
    *   M - K - X - G - H - B
    *   M - K - F - G - H - B
    All paths in this category pass through nodes K, G, and H in sequence (though not necessarily directly adjacent, but these are common intermediate nodes). Specifically, all paths in this category must pass through H to reach B.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    *   To disconnect M and B, we must break all paths from Category 1 AND all paths from Category 2.
    *   To break all paths in Category 1, we must remove node E.
    *   To break all paths in Category 2, we must remove a node that is common to all of them. Looking at the paths M - K - X - G - H - B and M - K - F - G - H - B, the common nodes (excluding M and B) are K, G, and H. Removing any one of these (K, G, or H) would break all paths in Category 2.

            *   L is connected only to M and E. Since E is removed, L is disconnected from B's side.
            *   K is connected to M, X, F.
                *   X is connected to K, G, F.
                *   F is connected to K, X, E. (E is removed)
                *   G is connected to X, F, H. (H is removed)
            *   From K, we can reach X and F. From X and F, we can reach G. However, G is only connected to X, F (since H is removed). There are no connections from X, F, or G to C or N (B's remaining neighbors).
    *   Thus, after removing E and H, no path exists between M and B.

Image name: PGS_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, G and I, without removing G or I themselves. This is a minimum vertex cut problem. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

I will systematically identify vertex-disjoint paths between G and I.

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**
    This is a direct path through node H.
    Intermediate nodes: {H}

2.  **Path 2: G - K - J - I**
    This path goes through nodes K and J.
    Intermediate nodes: {K, J}

3.  **Path 3: G - F - M - L - E - B - N - Y - O - P - I**
    This is a longer path. Let's list its intermediate nodes: {F, M, L, E, B, N, Y, O, P}

Now, let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes).
-   Intermediate nodes of Path 1: {H}
-   Intermediate nodes of Path 2: {K, J}
-   Intermediate nodes of Path 3: {F, M, L, E, B, N, Y, O, P}

These three sets of intermediate nodes are indeed disjoint. Since we have found three vertex-disjoint paths between G and I, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3. To do this, I can select one intermediate node from each of these three disjoint paths.

Let's choose the following nodes:
-   From Path 1: **H**
-   From Path 2: **J** (or K)
-   From Path 3: **F** (or M, L, E, B, N, Y, O, P)

So, a candidate cut set is {H, J, F}.

Let's verify that removing these three nodes (H, J, F) completely disconnects G from I:

1.  **Removing H**: This breaks Path 1 (G-H-I) and any other path that uses H (e.g., G-H-B-C-I, G-H-B-N-Y-O-P-I).
2.  **Removing J**: This breaks Path 2 (G-K-J-I) and any other path that uses J (e.g., G-X-K-J-I, G-F-K-J-I, G-K-J-P-I, G-K-J-D-P-I).
3.  **Removing F**: This breaks Path 3 (G-F-M-L-E-B-N-Y-O-P-I) and any other path that starts with G-F (e.g., G-F-M-L-E-B-C-I).

After removing F, H, and J:
-   The direct neighbors of G are F, H, X, K. Since F and H are removed, G can only reach X or K.
-   Node X is connected to F, K, G. Since F is removed, X can only reach K or G.
-   Node K is connected to F, J, X, G. Since F and J are removed, K can only reach X or G.

This means that after removing F, H, and J, the only nodes reachable from G are X and K, and X and K are only connected to each other and G. This forms an isolated component {G, X, K}.
The target node I and its remaining neighbors (P, C, D, O, Y, N, B, E, L, M) are in a separate component.
Therefore, no path exists between G and I after removing {F, H, J}.

Since we identified 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3. We have found a cut set of size 3 that achieves this disconnection.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [F, H, J]

    *   Node H is directly connected to I, X, and G.
    *   Therefore, any path from H must start with one of these three edges: (H, I), (H, X), or (H, G).

3.  **Analyze connections to E:**
    *   Node E is directly connected to B, F, and L.
    *   Therefore, any path to E must end with one of these three edges: (B, E), (F, E), or (L, E).

    *   **Paths starting with (H, I):**
        *   H-I-C-B-E
        *   H-I-C-N-B-E
        *   H-I-C-P-O-B-E
        *   H-I-C-P-O-N-B-E
        *   H-I-J-M-O-B-E
        *   H-I-J-M-L-E
        *   H-I-J-P-O-B-E
        *   H-I-J-P-C-B-E
        *   H-I-J-P-C-N-B-E
        All these paths eventually reach E via either (B, E) or (L, E).

    *   **Paths starting with (H, X):**
        *   H-X-K-F-E
        *   H-X-G-F-E
        Both these paths eventually reach E via (F, E).

    *   **Paths starting with (H, G):**
        *   H-G-F-E
        *   H-G-K-F-E
        Both these paths eventually reach E via (F, E).

5.  **Identify a potential minimal cut set:**
    *   To disconnect H from E, we need to break all paths.
    *   Consider removing the edge (H, I). This immediately breaks all paths in the "Paths starting with (H, I)" category (all paths that lead to E via B or L).
    *   After removing (H, I), the only remaining paths from H to E are those starting with (H, X) or (H, G).
    *   As observed in step 4, all paths starting with (H, X) or (H, G) eventually lead to E through the edge (F, E).
    *   Therefore, if we also remove the edge (F, E), all remaining paths will be broken.
    *   This gives us a cut set: [(H, I), (F, E)]. This set contains 2 edges.

6.  **Verify the cut set and its minimality:**
    *   **Verification:**
        *   Removing (H, I) ensures that H cannot reach I, and thus cannot reach E via any path that goes through I (which covers all paths to B-E and L-E).
        *   Removing (F, E) ensures that H cannot reach E via F, thus breaking all paths that go through X or G to F and then to E.
        *   With both edges removed, no path exists between H and E.
    *   **Minimality:**
        *   Could we disconnect H and E by removing only one edge?
        *   If we remove (H, I), paths like H-X-K-F-E and H-G-F-E still exist.
        *   If we remove (F, E), paths like H-I-C-B-E and H-I-J-M-L-E still exist.
        *   If we remove any other single edge, there will always be at least two distinct paths remaining (e.g., one through I and one through X/G, or one through B/L and one through F).
        *   Since no single edge can disconnect H and E, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.
        *   Since we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, I), (F, E)]

    *   If I remove P: Paths P1, P2, P3, P5, P7, P10 remain.
    *   If I remove Y: All paths listed above (P1-P10) remain, as none of them use Y.
    Since removing any single node does not disconnect H from O, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Propose a Two-Node Cut Set:**
    Consider the nodes that are direct predecessors to O in the paths: C and P. All paths from H to O must pass through either C or P (or Y, but Y is not a bottleneck for all paths).
    Let's test the cut set {C, P}.

7.  **Verify Cut Set {C, P}:**
    If nodes C and P are removed:
    *   All paths ending in C-O (P1, P2, P3, P5, P7, P10) are broken because C is removed.
    *   All paths ending in P-O (P4, P6, P8, P9) are broken because P is removed.

    *   Therefore, removing C and P successfully disconnects H from O.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are P and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect P from N. I cannot remove P or N.

First, I will identify all possible paths from P to N. To simplify, I will look for node-disjoint paths, as Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two nodes (the vertex connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them.

Let's find node-disjoint paths from P to N:

1.  **Path 1: P - O - N**
    *   This is a direct path. The intermediate node is O.

2.  **Path 2: P - I - C - N**
    *   This path uses intermediate nodes I and C.
    *   Let's check if it's node-disjoint from Path 1 (P-O-N). The intermediate nodes {I, C} are distinct from {O}. So, these two paths are node-disjoint (except for P and N).

3.  **Path 3: P - J - M - L - E - B - N**
    *   This path uses intermediate nodes J, M, L, E, B.
    *   Let's check if it's node-disjoint from Path 1 (P-O-N). The intermediate nodes {J, M, L, E, B} are distinct from {O}.
    *   Let's check if it's node-disjoint from Path 2 (P-I-C-N). The intermediate nodes {J, M, L, E, B} are distinct from {I, C}.
    *   Therefore, Path 3 is node-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2 (except for P and N).

I have found three node-disjoint paths between P and N:
*   Path 1: P - O - N (Intermediate node: O)
*   Path 2: P - I - C - N (Intermediate nodes: I, C)
*   Path 3: P - J - M - L - E - B - N (Intermediate nodes: J, M, L, E, B)

Since there are three node-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect P and N.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects P and N.
I observe that node N is directly connected to nodes C, O, and B. If all these three nodes are removed, N will have no connections to the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from P.

Let's verify this cut set: [C, O, B].
*   Removing O breaks Path 1 (P-O-N) and Path 4 (P-J-O-N).
*   Removing C breaks Path 2 (P-I-C-N) and Path 5 (P-J-I-C-N).
*   Removing B breaks Path 3 (P-J-M-L-E-B-N), Path 6 (P-J-I-H-G-F-E-B-N), and Path 7 (P-I-H-G-F-E-B-N).

Since N's only neighbors are C, O, and B, removing these three nodes will isolate N from the entire graph, ensuring no path exists between P and N.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and the cut set is [C, O, B].

    *   Neighbors of O: C, N, P, Y
3.  **Initial Assessment of Cut Set Size:**
    *   Any path from H to O must pass through one of H's neighbors and one of O's neighbors.
    *   Consider the nodes directly connected to O (excluding Y, which is a leaf node from O's perspective for paths originating from H): C, N, P.
    *   If we remove all three nodes {C, N, P}, then O will be completely disconnected from the rest of the graph (as Y is only connected to N and O, and N would be removed).
    *   Therefore, {C, N, P} is a valid cut set of size 3. This establishes an upper bound for the minimum number of nodes to remove.

4.  **Attempt to Find a Cut Set of Size 1:**
    *   Removing any single node (e.g., B, I, C, N, P, E, J) will leave multiple paths between H and O. For example:
        *   If B is removed: H - I - C - O still exists.
        *   If I is removed: H - B - C - O still exists.
        *   If C is removed: H - B - N - O and H - I - P - O still exist.
        *   If N is removed: H - B - C - O and H - I - P - O still exist.
        *   If P is removed: H - B - C - O and H - I - C - O and H - B - N - O still exist.
    *   Thus, a single node removal is not sufficient.

5.  **Attempt to Find a Cut Set of Size 2:**
    *   We need to systematically check if any pair of nodes can disconnect H and O.
    *   **Consider pairs from {C, N, P} (nodes directly connected to O):**
        *   **Remove {C, N}:** Node P is still connected to O. Paths from H to P still exist (e.g., H - I - P - O). So, {C, N} is not a cut set.
        *   **Remove {C, P}:** Node N is still connected to O. Paths from H to N still exist (e.g., H - B - N - O). So, {C, P} is not a cut set.
        *   **Remove {N, P}:** Node C is still connected to O. Paths from H to C still exist (e.g., H - B - C - O). So, {N, P} is not a cut set.
    *   This proves that removing any two of the nodes directly connected to O is not sufficient.

    *   **Consider pairs of "bottleneck" nodes that connect the H-side to the O-side (e.g., B, I, E, J):**
        *   **Remove {B, I}:**
            *   Paths through B are broken.
            *   Paths through I are broken.
            *   However, the path H - G - F - E - J - P - O still exists. (None of G, F, E, J, P are B or I). So, {B, I} is not a cut set.
        *   **Remove {B, E}:**
            *   Paths through B are broken.
            *   Paths through E are broken.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (the edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Therefore, I will search for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths from E to J.

3.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - J
        *   Edges: (E, F), (F, K), (K, J)
    *   **Path 3:** E - G - H - I - J
        *   Edges: (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

4.  **Verify edge-disjoint property:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (E, F), (F, K), (K, J)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   Edges in Path 4: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)
    Upon inspection, all the edges listed across these four paths are unique and do not overlap. Therefore, these are indeed four edge-disjoint paths between E and J.

5.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:** Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, according to Menger's theorem, at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.

6.  **Identify a cut set of size 4:** To achieve disconnection, I need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal breaks all paths. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that lead to the other target node's "side" of the graph.
    *   Node J is directly connected to M, K, I, P, and D.
    *   Node D is not connected to any node reachable from E.
    *   Therefore, removing the edges (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), and (P, J) will disconnect J from all nodes that E can reach.
    *   This set of edges, [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)], has a size of 4.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 4 edge-disjoint paths (implying a minimum cut of at least 4) and identified a cut set of exactly 4 edges that successfully disconnects E and J, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: F, L, M, B, I.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, D, P, I.

2.  **Analyze the structure and potential bottlenecks:**
    All paths from E to J must eventually pass through one of J's direct neighbors (M, D, P, I) and use the corresponding edge to J.

3.  **Consider the edges directly connected to J:**
    The edges connected to J are (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), and (J, I). If we remove all four of these edges, J would be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. This would be a cut of size 4. We need to check if a smaller cut is possible.

4.  **Look for edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem intuition):**
    If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths between E and J, then the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least *k*. Let's try to find such paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
        *   Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - I - J
        *   Edges: {(E, I), (I, J)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        *   Edges: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses (E, M) and (M, J).
    *   Path 2 uses (E, I) and (I, J).
    *   Path 3 uses (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J).
    Indeed, these three paths do not share any common edges.

    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Based on the observation that all paths to J must use one of its direct edges, let's try to cut these "entry points" to J.
    *   To cut all paths going through M to J, we can remove edge (M, J). This covers paths like E-M-J and E-L-M-J.
    *   To cut all paths going through I to J, we can remove edge (I, J). This covers paths like E-I-J, E-B-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-F-G-H-B-I-J, etc.
    *   To cut all paths going through P to J, we can remove edge (P, J). This covers paths like E-B-C-O-P-J and E-B-C-N-O-P-J.

    What about node D? Node D is only connected to J and P. If (P, J) is removed, then P cannot reach J. If D is to reach J, it must use (D, J). However, D itself can only be reached from E via P (e.g., E -> ... -> P -> D). If (P, J) is removed, then any path E -> ... -> P -> D -> J is also effectively cut because P cannot reach J directly, and D's only other connection to J is (D, J), but D cannot be reached from E without passing through P. More simply, if (P, J) is removed, then P cannot reach J. Since D is only connected to J and P, and P cannot reach J, D cannot serve as a bridge from E to J.

    Therefore, the cut set **[(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]** effectively disconnects E from J.

6.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   Removing (M, J) breaks all paths from E to J that pass through M.
    *   Removing (I, J) breaks all paths from E to J that pass through I.
    *   Removing (P, J) breaks all paths from E to J that pass through P. Since D is only connected to J and P, and P's connection to J is cut, D cannot provide an alternative path from E to J.

    This cut set has 3 edges. Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this set is minimal.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)]

        *   Path 9: G - B - C - O - P - I
        *   Path 10: G - B - N - Y - O - P - I

    *   Path A: G - H - I (Edges: (G, H), (H, I))
    *   Path B: G - E - M - J - I (Edges: (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I))
    *   Path C: G - B - C - I (Edges: (G, B), (B, C), (C, I))

    These three paths (A, B, C) are edge-disjoint, meaning they do not share any common edges. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G and I must be at least the number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 3.

    *   To cut all paths through B (Paths 6-10), I can remove the edge **(G, B)**. This edge is the first step for all these paths.

    *   Removing (M, J) breaks Paths 2, 3, 4, 5.
    *   Removing (G, B) breaks Paths 6, 7, 8, 9, 10.

    Since all identified paths from G to I are covered by the removal of these three edges, G and I will be completely disconnected.

I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to H and B:**
    *   H is connected to G, I, and B.
    *   B is connected to H, E, C, and N.

        H-I-C-B
        Edges used: `(H,I)`, `(I,C)`, `(C,B)`
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

    P1: H-B
    P2: H-G-F-E-B
    P3: H-I-C-B

3.  **Check for a fourth edge-disjoint path:**
    All edges directly connected to H (`(H,B)`, `(H,G)`, `(H,I)`) have been used in P1, P2, and P3 respectively. Therefore, no other path can originate from H without using an already used edge. This means there are at most 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H from B is 3.

5.  **Identify a minimal cut set of edges:**
    To disconnect H from B, I need to cut at least one edge from each of these three edge-disjoint paths. A straightforward way to do this is to select one edge from each path such that their removal breaks all paths.

    *   `(H,B)` (from P1)
    *   `(E,B)` (from P2, this edge is the final connection to B for all paths coming from G via E)
    *   `(H,I)` (from P3, this edge is the initial connection from H for all paths going through I)

    *   Removing `(H,B)` breaks the direct path.
    *   Removing `(E,B)` breaks all paths from H that go through G and eventually reach E to connect to B (e.g., H-G-F-E-B, H-G-X-F-E-B, H-G-X-K-M-E-B). It also breaks paths from H through I that eventually reach E (e.g., H-I-J-M-E-B).

*   To break Channel 1, I must remove node **B**.
*   To break Channel 2, I must remove node **F**.
*   To break Channel 3, I must remove either node **P** or node **J**.

*   If I remove F and P (or J): Channel 2 and Channel 3 are broken. However, paths like H - B - E (from Channel 1) would still exist.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are G and N. My task is to find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect G and N.

First, I identify all possible paths from G to N. I can categorize these paths based on the immediate neighbors of G: F, H, K, X.

**Category 1: Paths starting with G -> H**
From H, there are two main branches:
*   **Branch 1a: G -> H -> B -> N** (This is a direct path to N's neighbor B).
*   **Branch 1b: G -> H -> I -> ... -> N** (I connects to C, J, P).
    *   G -> H -> I -> C -> N
    *   G -> H -> I -> C -> B -> N
    *   G -> H -> I -> C -> O -> N
    *   G -> H -> I -> J -> P -> O -> N
    *   G -> H -> I -> J -> D -> P -> O -> N
    *   G -> H -> I -> P -> O -> N
All paths in Category 1 (Branch 1a and 1b) pass through node **H**. Therefore, removing H would sever all these paths.

**Category 2: Paths starting with G -> F, G -> K, or G -> X**
Nodes F, K, and X are all interconnected and also connected to G. Let's trace how paths from this cluster reach N.
*   F is connected to E, M, L.
*   K is connected to F, X.
*   X is connected to F, K.
*   M is connected to F, E, L.
*   L is connected to F, E, M.

Observing the connections, all nodes in the {F, K, X, M, L} cluster are connected to node **E**.
*   F -> E
*   M -> E
*   L -> E
And node E is connected to node B, which is a neighbor of N.
So, any path from G that goes through F, K, or X must eventually pass through E to reach B and then N. Examples:
*   G -> F -> E -> B -> N
*   G -> F -> M -> E -> B -> N
*   G -> K -> F -> E -> B -> N
*   G -> X -> F -> E -> B -> N
Therefore, removing node **E** would sever all paths in Category 2.

**Identifying the Minimum Cut Set:**
We have identified that:
1.  All paths in Category 1 (starting G -> H) are cut by removing **H**.
2.  All paths in Category 2 (starting G -> F, G -> K, or G -> X) are cut by removing **E**.

Since these two categories cover all possible paths from G to N, removing both H and E will completely disconnect G from N. This gives us a cut set of size 2: [E, H].

**Verifying Minimality:**
To confirm that 2 is the minimum, I must check if any single node removal can disconnect G and N.
*   If I remove only H, paths like G-F-E-B-N still exist.
*   If I remove only E, paths like G-H-B-N or G-H-I-C-N still exist.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., B, C, I, O, P, J, D, F, K, X, M, L, Y), there will always be at least one path remaining. For example, removing B would cut G-H-B-N and G-F-E-B-N, but G-H-I-C-N would still exist.

Since no single node can disconnect G and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2. Our identified cut set [E, H] has size 2, which is therefore the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

    *   Node H is connected to: C, I, G.

2.  **Analyze the structure around node H:**

3.  **Consider a potential cut set by isolating H:**
    If we remove all edges directly connected to H, namely (C, H), (I, H), and (G, H), then H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path will exist between B and H. This set of edges constitutes a valid cut set. The size of this cut set is 3.

4.  **Check for minimality (can we do it with fewer edges?):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that it's impossible to disconnect B and H by removing only 1 or 2 edges. This can be done by finding edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If there are 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' edges must be removed.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:
    *   **Path 1:** B - C - H
        Edges: {(B, C), (C, H)}
    *   **Path 2:** B - I - H
        Edges: {(B, I), (I, H)}
    *   **Path 3:** B - E - P - F - G - H
        Edges: {(B, E), (E, P), (P, F), (F, G), (G, H)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The edges in Path 1 are (B,C) and (C,H).
    *   The edges in Path 2 are (B,I) and (I,H).
    *   The edges in Path 3 are (B,E), (E,P), (P,F), (F,G), (G,H).

    Comparing the edge sets, there are no common edges between any two of these three paths. For example, (B,C) is unique to Path 1, (B,I) to Path 2, and (B,E) to Path 3. Similarly, (C,H) is unique to Path 1, (I,H) to Path 2, and (G,H) to Path 3. All intermediate edges in Path 3 are also unique to that path.

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between B and H, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    We have identified a cut set of 3 edges [(C, H), (I, H), (G, H)] that disconnects B and H, and we have proven that no fewer than 3 edges can achieve this.

    The edges to remove would be: (M, L), (M, E), (M, F), (M, J).
    This set contains 4 edges. This is a valid cut set.

    If we remove all edges directly connected to B, B will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from M.
    The edges to remove would be: (E, B), (G, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B).
    This set contains 5 edges. This is a valid cut set.

5.  **Determine an upper bound for the minimum cut:**

6.  **Check if a cut of size 3 is possible:**

    Let's consider the edges directly connected to M: (M, L), (M, E), (M, F), (M, J).
    If we remove any 3 of these 4 edges, one edge will always remain connected to M.
    *   If (M, L) remains: A path M-L-E-B exists (since E is connected to B).
    *   If (M, E) remains: A path M-E-B exists.

    *   If (M, J) remains: A path M-J-I-H-B exists (since J is connected to I, I to H, and H to B).
    In all cases, if only 3 edges directly connected to M are removed, a path from M to B still exists. Therefore, removing 3 edges directly connected to M is not sufficient.

    Similarly, let's consider the edges directly connected to B: (E, B), (G, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B).
    If we remove any 3 of these 5 edges, at least two edges will remain connected to B.
    For example, if we remove (E, B), (G, B), (H, B), then (C, B) and (N, B) remain.
    Paths like M-J-I-C-B and M-J-P-O-N-B would still exist.
    Therefore, removing 3 edges directly connected to B is not sufficient.

    It is generally more complex to prove that no combination of 3 "intermediate" edges works without a formal algorithm (like max-flow min-cut, which would show that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 4). However, given the structure and the fact that cutting all edges from M (a source node) is a valid cut of size 4, and we've shown that 3 edges directly connected to M or B are insufficient, it strongly suggests that 4 is the minimum.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    Since a cut of 4 edges is sufficient, and we've reasoned that 3 edges are not sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4. The cut set consists of all edges directly connected to node M.

- Paths through B are broken by (B,N).
- Paths through C are broken because O (the only node connecting C to the rest of the graph besides N) is disconnected from P, which is the gateway from H's side.

- If I remove only (B,N), paths through C and Y (e.g., H-I-P-O-C-N) remain.
- If I remove only (P,O), paths through B (e.g., H-B-N) remain.
Since no single edge can disconnect H and N, the minimum number of edges is 2.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, J and F.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are J and F.

2.  **Consider direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node J is connected to M, D, P, and I. (Degree of J is 4)
    *   Node F is connected to E, K, X, and G. (Degree of F is 4)

3.  **Initial Cut Set Candidates:**
    *   A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges connected to it.
    *   Removing all edges connected to J: {(J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I)}. This is a cut set of size 4.
    *   Removing all edges connected to F: {(F, E), (F, K), (F, X), (F, G)}. This is a cut set of size 4.
    *   Both of these cut sets successfully disconnect J from F. The question is whether a smaller cut (e.g., size 3 or less) exists.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** Menger's theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them. If we can find 4 edge-disjoint paths between J and F, then the minimum cut size must be at least 4. Since we already found a cut of size 4, this would prove it is the minimum.

5.  **Search for Edge-Disjoint Paths:** Let's try to find four paths from J to F that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): J - M - L - E - F**
        Edges: {(J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): J - I - G - F**
        Edges: {(J, I), (I, G), (G, F)}

    *   **Path 3 (P3): J - D - P - C - H - X - F**
        Edges: {(J, D), (D, P), (P, C), (C, H), (H, X), (X, F)}

    *   **Path 4 (P4): J - P - O - B - H - G - K - F**
        Edges: {(J, P), (P, O), (O, B), (B, H), (H, G), (G, K), (K, F)}

6.  **Verify Edge Disjointness:** Let's list all edges from these four paths and check for any overlaps:
    *   P1 Edges: (J,M), (M,L), (L,E), (E,F)
    *   P2 Edges: (J,I), (I,G), (G,F)
    *   P3 Edges: (J,D), (D,P), (P,C), (C,H), (H,X), (X,F)
    *   P4 Edges: (J,P), (P,O), (O,B), (B,H), (H,G), (G,K), (K,F)

    Upon careful inspection, all edges listed above are unique to their respective paths. There are no shared edges between any pair of these four paths. Therefore, these are 4 edge-disjoint paths from J to F.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    *   Since we found 4 edge-disjoint paths between J and F, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect J and F is at least 4.
    *   We have also identified a cut set of size 4: {(F, E), (F, K), (F, X), (F, G)}. Removing these four edges isolates node F from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from J.
    *   Since the minimum required is 4 and we found a cut of size 4, this is the minimal number of edges.

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and D. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from D.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node F is connected to E, X, and K.
    *   Node D is connected to J and P.

2.  **Consider the degree of the target nodes:**
    *   Node D has a degree of 2, meaning it is directly connected to two other nodes (J and P).
    *   To completely disconnect node D from the rest of the graph, both of its incident edges must be removed. If only one edge is removed, a path to D would still exist through the other edge.

3.  **Propose a cut set based on D's connections:**
    *   If we remove the edges (D, J) and (D, P), node D becomes isolated.
    *   An isolated node cannot be part of any path to or from any other node, including F.
    *   Therefore, removing these two edges would successfully disconnect F from D.
    *   This cut set has a size of 2: [(D, J), (D, P)].

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Can we disconnect F and D by removing only one edge?
        *   If we remove (D, J), paths to D via P would still exist (e.g., F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-D).
        *   If we remove (D, P), paths to D via J would still exist (e.g., F-E-M-J-D).
    *   Since removing a single edge is insufficient, the minimum number of edges required must be at least 2.
    *   As we found a cut set of size 2 that successfully disconnects F from D, this is the minimum.

5.  **Consider alternative cut sets of size 2 (to confirm no other obvious minimal cut):**
    *   Let's consider the "entry points" to D's immediate neighborhood: J and P.
    *   Paths to J come from M and H. Paths to P come from O and I.
    *   If we try to cut edges further away, for example, by removing (M, J) and (O, P):
        *   Paths like F-E-B-H-J-D would still exist (via H to J).
        *   Paths like F-E-B-N-C-I-P-D would still exist (via I to P).
        *   So, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.
    *   Similarly, removing (H, J) and (I, P) would leave paths like F-E-M-J-D and F-E-B-N-Y-O-P-D.
    *   This reinforces that the direct connections to D provide the most straightforward and provably minimal cut.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(D, J), (D, P)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['D', 'P'], ['D', 'J']]}

The target nodes are J (black) and E (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that, when removed, will completely disconnect J from E.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to node J:**
    Node J is connected to three other nodes:
    *   (J, M)
    *   (J, D)
    *   (J, P)

2.  **Consider isolating node J:**
    If I remove all edges directly connected to node J, then J will become an isolated node. An isolated node cannot have any paths to any other node in the graph, including E.
    Removing the edges (J, M), (J, D), and (J, P) would isolate J. This set of edges has a size of 3.

3.  **Verify if this cut set disconnects J from E:**
    Yes, if (J, M), (J, D), and (J, P) are removed, J has no remaining connections. Therefore, no path can exist from J to E.

4.  **Check for a smaller cut (fewer than 3 edges):**
    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect J from E?** No. If I remove any single edge (e.g., (J, M)), J would still be connected to P and D, and from there, paths to E would still exist (e.g., J-P-I-C-B-E).
    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect J from E?** No.
        *   If I remove any two edges directly connected to J (e.g., (J, M) and (J, D)), J would still be connected via the third edge (J, P). From P, there are multiple paths to E (e.g., P-I-C-B-E, P-O-Y-N-B-E, P-I-H-G-K-X-F-E). So, J would still be connected to E.
        *   If I try to cut edges further away from J, for example, (M, E) and (I, H), then:
            *   The path J-M-E is cut by (M, E).
            *   The path J-P-I-H-...-E is cut by (I, H).
            *   However, paths like J-P-I-C-B-E, J-P-O-C-B-E, and J-P-O-Y-N-B-E would still exist, connecting J to E.
        Therefore, removing only 2 edges is not sufficient to disconnect J from E.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 2 edges are not enough, and 3 edges (specifically, the ones directly connected to J) are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, P)]

Step-by-step reasoning.

    Node F is directly connected to nodes K, X, G, and E.
    Therefore, any path originating from F must start with one of these four edges: (F, K), (F, X), (F, G), or (F, E).

2.  **Analyze the connectivity of F's neighbors:**
    *   K is connected to F, X, G.
    *   X is connected to F, K, G.
    *   G is connected to F, K, X, H.
    *   E is connected to F, M, B, L.

3.  **Determine the "gateways" from F to the rest of the graph leading to J:**
    *   From F, we can reach G through the edges (F, G), (F, K) then (K, G), or (F, X) then (X, G). All paths from F that go through K or X eventually lead to G (e.g., F-K-G, F-X-G). So, G acts as a central point for paths originating from F via K or X.
    *   From F, we can reach E directly via (F, E).

    This means that all paths from F to any other node in the graph (and thus to J) must pass through either node G or node E.

4.  **Consider potential cut sets:**
    *   **Cut of size 1?**
        If we remove only one edge, for example (F, G), then paths like F-E-M-J would still exist.
        If we remove only one edge, for example (F, E), then paths like F-G-H-I-J would still exist.
        If we remove (F, K) or (F, X), paths through (F, G) and (F, E) would still exist.
        Therefore, a single edge removal is insufficient to disconnect F from J. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

    *   **Cut of size 2?**
        Based on the analysis in step 3, if we remove the edges (F, G) and (F, E), we effectively sever all connections from F to the rest of the graph that could lead to J.
        *   Removing (F, G) breaks all paths that start with F-G, F-K-G, or F-X-G. Since K and X are only connected to F and G (and each other), removing (F,G) effectively isolates K and X from the rest of the graph (except F).
        *   Removing (F, E) breaks all paths that start with F-E.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set [(F, G), (F, E)]:**
    *   Any path from F to J must begin with an edge from F.
    *   If (F, G) is removed, F cannot reach G. Since K and X are only connected to F and G (and each other), they cannot serve as alternative routes to J once (F,G) is removed.
    *   If (F, E) is removed, F cannot reach E.
    *   Since all paths from F to J must pass through either G or E, removing both (F, G) and (F, E) ensures that F is completely disconnected from J.

This cut set of 2 edges is sufficient, and we've established that 1 edge is not enough. Therefore, 2 is the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, G), (F, E)]
