Image name: PG_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are J and E. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J from E.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are J and E.

2.  **Strategy: Find edge-disjoint paths.** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. I will try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible from J to E.

3.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    *   **Path 1:** J -> M -> L -> E
        *   Edges: (J,M), (M,L), (L,E)
    *   **Path 2:** J -> D -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E
        *   Edges: (J,D), (D,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B), (B,E)
    *   **Path 3:** J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> F -> E
        *   Edges: (J,I), (I,H), (H,G), (G,K), (K,F), (F,E)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    Let's list all edges from these three paths and check for any overlaps:
    *   Edges from Path 1: {(J,M), (M,L), (L,E)}
    *   Edges from Path 2: {(J,D), (D,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B), (B,E)}
    *   Edges from Path 3: {(J,I), (I,H), (H,G), (G,K), (K,F), (F,E)}

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these three paths are distinct. For example, the starting edges from J are (J,M), (J,D), and (J,I), which are all different. The ending edges connected to E are (L,E), (B,E), and (F,E), which are also all different. No edge is shared between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect J from E.

6.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Consider the edges directly connected to node E: (E,B), (E,L), and (E,F).
    *   Removing (E,L) breaks Path 1 (J-M-L-E).
    *   Removing (E,B) breaks Path 2 (J-D-P-O-N-B-E) and any other path that reaches E via B.
    *   Removing (E,F) breaks Path 3 (J-I-H-G-K-F-E) and any other path that reaches E via F.

    By removing these three edges, node E becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path exists between J and E. This cut set has a size of 3.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning at least 3 edges must be removed) and we identified a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects J and E, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, L), (E, F)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']]}

The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I. I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

First, I identify all possible paths between G and I. To do this systematically, I'll look for paths that are edge-disjoint.

1.  **Path 1: G - E - I**
    Edges: (G, E), (E, I)

2.  **Path 2: G - H - B - C - I**
    Edges: (G, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, I)
    Let's check if Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.
    (G, H) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
    (H, B) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
    (B, C) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
    (C, I) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
    Yes, Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: G - F - E - M - J - I**
    Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)
    Let's check if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
    *   Compared to Path 1 (edges: (G, E), (E, I)):
        (G, F) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
        (F, E) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
        (E, M) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
        (M, J) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
        (J, I) is not (G, E) or (E, I).
        Yes, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.
    *   Compared to Path 2 (edges: (G, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, I)):
        (G, F) is not any of Path 2's edges.
        (F, E) is not any of Path 2's edges.
        (E, M) is not any of Path 2's edges.
        (M, J) is not any of Path 2's edges.
        (J, I) is not any of Path 2's edges.
        Yes, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 2.

Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between G and I, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect G from I.
Consider the "cut" that separates the nodes directly connected to G (G, K, X, F) from the rest of the graph that leads to I. The edges that cross this boundary are:
*   (G, E)
*   (G, H)
*   (F, E)

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects G from I:
*   Any path starting with G-E is broken by removing (G, E). This includes Path 1 (G-E-I).
*   Any path starting with G-H is broken by removing (G, H). This includes Path 2 (G-H-B-C-I).
*   Any path starting with G-F must use F. From F, the only way to reach the "I-side" of the graph is through E via the edge (F, E). By removing (F, E), all paths originating from G-F (including those from G-K-F and G-X-F) are blocked from reaching E and thus I. This includes Path 3 (G-F-E-M-J-I).

Since these three edges effectively cut all identified edge-disjoint paths and all other possible paths from G to I, and we have established that at least 3 edges are required, this is the minimal cut set.

Final Answer:

        *   Removing [E, O]: Paths N-B-C-I-P and N-C-I-P remain.
        *   Removing [C, J]: Paths N-B-E-G-H-I-P (and variations) and N-Y-O-P remain.
    *   Through systematic exploration, it becomes clear that no pair of nodes can disconnect N and P. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

7.  **Attempt to find a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    *   Consider removing all direct neighbors of N: [B, C, Y]. If these three nodes are removed, N has no remaining connections to any other white node, thus it is completely disconnected from P. This is a valid cut set.
    *   Consider removing all effective gateways to P: [I, O, J]. If these three nodes are removed, P has no remaining connections to any other white node (D is also cut off from N's side as it relies on J). Thus, P is completely disconnected from N. This is a valid cut set.
    *   Another example: [E, C, O].
        *   Removing E breaks all paths that go N-B-E-... (e.g., N-B-E-L-M-J-P, N-B-E-G-H-I-P).
        *   Removing C breaks all paths that go N-C-... (e.g., N-C-I-P, N-C-O-P) and N-B-C-... (e.g., N-B-C-I-P, N-B-C-O-P).
        *   Removing O breaks the path N-Y-O-P.
        *   With E, C, and O removed, all identified paths from N to P are broken. This is a valid cut set.

Since we have found multiple cut sets of size 3, and have systematically ruled out any smaller cut sets, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, C, Y]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B and H.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to H:**
    Node H is directly connected to three other nodes: B, G, and I.
    The edges incident to H are: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

2.  **Consider the effect of removing these direct edges:**
    If we remove all three edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H), then node H will have no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between B and H.
    This set of edges, {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)}, constitutes a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 1 or 2) exists:**

    *   **Can we disconnect B and H by removing only 1 edge?**
        No. If we remove (B, H), paths like B-E-F-G-H and B-C-I-H still exist. If we remove (G, H), paths like B-H and B-C-I-H still exist. If we remove (I, H), paths like B-H and B-E-F-G-H still exist. Therefore, 1 edge is not enough.

    *   **Can we disconnect B and H by removing only 2 edges?**
        Let's consider all possible scenarios for removing two edges, {e1, e2}:

        *   **Scenario A: Both e1 and e2 are from the set of edges directly connected to H, i.e., {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)}.**
            *   If we remove (B, H) and (G, H): The path B-C-I-H (and others through I) still exists because (I, H) is intact.
            *   If we remove (B, H) and (I, H): The path B-E-F-G-H (and others through G) still exists because (G, H) is intact.
            *   If we remove (G, H) and (I, H): The direct path B-H still exists because (B, H) is intact.
            In all these cases, a path between B and H remains. So, removing any two edges directly connected to H is not sufficient.

        *   **Scenario B: One edge is (B, H), and the other edge (e2) is not directly connected to H.**
            If (B, H) is removed, we still need to break all paths from B to H that go through G and all paths from B to H that go through I. This means e2 must simultaneously cut all paths from B to G AND all paths from B to I.
            *   Paths from B to G: All paths from B to G (e.g., B-E-F-G, B-E-L-M-F-G, etc.) must pass through node E. So, to cut all paths to G, e2 would need to be (B, E) or (E, F).
            *   Paths from B to I: All paths from B to I (e.g., B-C-I, B-N-C-I, B-C-O-P-I, B-N-C-O-P-I, B-N-Y-O-P-I) must pass through either C or N. To cut all paths to I, we would need to remove at least two edges (e.g., (B, C) and (B, N), or (C, I) and (P, I)).
            Since a single edge e2 cannot cut all paths to G and all paths to I simultaneously (it would require at least one edge for G-paths and at least two edges for I-paths, totaling 3 edges), this scenario cannot achieve disconnection with only 2 edges.

        *   **Scenario C: Neither e1 nor e2 is (B, H).**
            If (B, H) is not removed, then the direct path B-H still exists. Therefore, B and H are still connected. This scenario cannot achieve disconnection.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Based on the analysis, it is impossible to disconnect B and H by removing only 1 or 2 edges. The minimum number of edges required is 3. The cut set identified in step 2, which removes all edges directly connected to H, achieves this minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are G and I. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G from I. I cannot remove G or I.

First, I identify all distinct paths from G to I:
1.  **G - H - I** (Direct path)
2.  **G - X - K - J - P - I** (Upper path via X, K, J, P)
3.  **G - X - K - J - M - L - E - B - C - I** (Upper path via X, K, J, M, L, E, B, C)
4.  **G - X - F - E - B - C - I** (Middle path via X, F, E, B, C)
5.  **G - X - F - E - M - J - P - I** (Middle path via X, F, E, M, J, P)
6.  **G - F - E - B - C - I** (Lower path via F, E, B, C)
7.  **G - F - E - M - J - P - I** (Lower path via F, E, M, J, P)

To disconnect G from I, all these paths must be broken. I will try to find the minimum number of nodes required.

**Attempting a cut set of size 1:**
No single node appears in all listed paths. For example:
*   Removing H breaks path 1, but paths 2-7 remain.
*   Removing J breaks paths 2, 3, 5, 7, but paths 1, 4, 6 remain.
*   Removing B breaks paths 3, 4, 6, but paths 1, 2, 5, 7 remain.
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

**Attempting a cut set of size 2:**
Let's assume a cut set of size 2, {N1, N2}, exists.
Observe path 1: G - H - I. For this path to be broken, either G, H, or I must be removed. Since G and I cannot be removed, H *must* be part of any cut set.
So, N1 must be H. Our cut set is now {H, N2}.

Now, with H removed, we need to find a single node N2 that can break all remaining paths:
*   Paths through J and P:
    *   G - X - K - J - P - I
    *   G - X - F - E - M - J - P - I
    *   G - F - E - M - J - P - I
*   Paths through E, B, C:
    *   G - X - K - J - M - L - E - B - C - I
    *   G - X - F - E - B - C - I
    *   G - F - E - B - C - I

Let's test possible candidates for N2:
*   If N2 = J: Paths through J and P are broken. However, paths like G - F - E - B - C - I (paths through E, B, C) remain connected. So {H, J} is not a cut set.
*   If N2 = P: Paths through J and P are broken. However, paths like G - F - E - B - C - I (paths through E, B, C) remain connected. So {H, P} is not a cut set.
*   If N2 = B: Paths through E, B, C are broken. However, paths like G - X - K - J - P - I (paths through J, P) remain connected. So {H, B} is not a cut set.
*   If N2 = C: Paths through E, B, C are broken. However, paths like G - X - K - J - P - I (paths through J, P) remain connected. So {H, C} is not a cut set.
*   If N2 = E: Paths through E, B, C are broken. However, paths like G - X - K - J - P - I (paths through J, P) remain connected. So {H, E} is not a cut set.
*   If N2 is any other node (X, K, F, M, L, D, N, O, Y), it will not break both the J/P-related paths and the E/B/C-related paths.

Since H must be in the cut set, and no single other node N2 can disconnect the remaining paths, a cut set of size 2 is impossible.

**Attempting a cut set of size 3:**
Based on the analysis above, we need to break three "channels" of paths:
1.  The direct path G-H-I.
2.  The upper paths that primarily go through J and P.
3.  The lower paths that primarily go through E, B, and C.

A possible cut set would be to pick one node from each channel:
*   From channel 1: H
*   From channel 2: J (or P)
*   From channel 3: B (or C or E)

Let's choose the set **{H, J, B}** and verify:
1.  **G - H - I**: Broken by removing H.
2.  **G - X - K - J - P - I**: Broken by removing J.
3.  **G - X - K - J - M - L - E - B - C - I**: Broken by removing J (or B).
4.  **G - X - F - E - B - C - I**: Broken by removing B.
5.  **G - X - F - E - M - J - P - I**: Broken by removing J.
6.  **G - F - E - B - C - I**: Broken by removing B.
7.  **G - F - E - M - J - P - I**: Broken by removing J.

All paths are successfully broken by removing nodes H, J, and B. Since we have proven that a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are M and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between M and N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node M (black) is connected to E, L, and J.
    *   Node N (black) is connected to B, C, O, and Y.

2.  **Analyze paths from M to N:**
    I need to find all possible paths from M to N. Let's list some representative paths:
    *   Path 1: M - E - H - B - N
    *   Path 2: M - E - H - I - C - N
    *   Path 3: M - E - H - I - P - O - N
    *   Path 4: M - J - I - C - N
    *   Path 5: M - J - I - H - B - N
    *   Path 6: M - J - I - P - O - N
    *   Path 7: M - J - P - O - N
    (There are also longer paths involving F, G, K, X, but they all pass through E or J first).

3.  **Identify initial branching points from M:**
    All paths originating from M towards N must start with either the edge (M, E) or the edge (M, J). The edge (M, L) leads to node L, which is only connected to E and M. Since E is part of the main graph leading to N, if (M, E) is cut, L cannot provide an alternative path to N.

4.  **Consider a cut of size 1:**
    If I remove only one edge, for example (M, E), then paths like M-J-I-C-N still exist. If I remove (M, J), paths like M-E-H-B-N still exist. Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect M from N. This means the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Consider a cut of size 2:**
    Since all paths from M to N must begin with either (M, E) or (M, J), removing both of these edges would effectively isolate M from the rest of the graph that can reach N.
    *   If I remove (M, E): All paths starting with M-E are broken.
    *   If I also remove (M, J): All paths starting with M-J are broken.
    After removing both (M, E) and (M, J), node M is only connected to node L. Node L is connected to E, but since (M, E) is removed, M cannot reach E via L. Node L itself is not connected to N or any path leading to N. Therefore, M is completely disconnected from N.

6.  **Verify minimality:**
    As established in step 4, a cut of size 1 is not possible. A cut of size 2, specifically [(M, E), (M, J)], successfully disconnects M from N. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(M, E), (M, J)]

Image name: Tu_task2_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and J. I cannot remove these nodes.
2.  **List all simple paths between E and J:**
    *   Path 1: E - M - J
    *   Path 2: E - P - J
    *   Path 3: E - B - C - I - J
    *   Path 4: E - B - H - I - J
    *   Other paths like E-B-C-N-O-P-J or E-B-C-O-P-J are covered by Path 2 because they pass through P. Similarly, E-L-M-J is covered by Path 1 because it passes through M.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut):**
    I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (s and t) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between s and t.

    Let's find vertex-disjoint paths between E and J:
    *   Path A: E - M - J (Intermediate node: M)
    *   Path B: E - P - J (Intermediate node: P)
    *   Path C: E - B - C - I - J (Intermediate nodes: B, C, I)

    Let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (excluding E and J):
    *   Path A uses M.
    *   Path B uses P.
    *   Path C uses B, C, I.
    All intermediate nodes (M, P, B, C, I) are distinct. Therefore, these three paths (A, B, C) are vertex-disjoint.

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between E and J, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Based on the identified paths, I need to find a set of 3 nodes that breaks all of them.
    Consider the set of nodes {M, P, I}:
    *   Removing M breaks Path 1 (E - M - J).
    *   Removing P breaks Path 2 (E - P - J) and any other path going through P (e.g., E-B-C-N-O-P-J).
    *   Removing I breaks Path 3 (E - B - C - I - J) and Path 4 (E - B - H - I - J).

    Let's verify that removing {M, P, I} completely disconnects E and J:
    *   E's remaining neighbors are L and B.
    *   J's remaining neighbors are D. (M, P, I are removed).
    *   From E:
        *   E - L: L is only connected to E and M. Since M is removed, L is a dead end for reaching J.
        *   E - B: B is connected to C, H, N, O.
            *   B - C: C is connected to N, O, H. (I is removed, so C cannot reach J via I).
            *   B - H: H is connected to C, G. (I is removed, so H cannot reach J via I).
            *   B - N: N is connected to C, O, Y. (P is removed, so N cannot reach J via O-P).
            *   B - O: O is connected to C, N, Y. (P is removed, so O cannot reach J via P).
    Since no path remains from E to J, the set {M, P, I} successfully disconnects E and J.

    Another valid cut set of size 3 would be {M, P, B}.
    *   Removing M breaks Path 1.
    *   Removing P breaks Path 2.
    *   Removing B breaks Path 3 and Path 4 (as both start with E-B).
    If M, P, B are removed, E is only connected to L. L is only connected to E and M (which is removed). So E is isolated from J.

Since I have found a cut set of size 3, and Menger's Theorem indicates that the minimum is at least 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, P, I]

Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My task is to find the minimum number of non-target nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them.

First, I will identify all direct neighbors of E and I to understand the immediate connections.
Neighbors of E: F, M, B
Neighbors of I: J, H, C, P

Next, I will systematically search for node-disjoint paths between E and I. Node-disjoint means that these paths do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than E and I themselves).

**Path 1:**
Starting from E, I can go to B. From B, I can go to C. From C, I can go to I.
E - B - C - I
Intermediate nodes for Path 1: {B, C}

**Path 2:**
Starting from E, I can go to M. From M, I can go to L. From L, I can go to J. From J, I can go to I.
E - M - L - J - I
Intermediate nodes for Path 2: {M, L, J}

**Path 3:**
Starting from E, I can go to F. From F, I can go to K. From K, I can go to G. From G, I can go to H. From H, I can go to I.
E - F - K - G - H - I
Intermediate nodes for Path 3: {F, K, G, H}

Now, I will check if these three paths are indeed node-disjoint (excluding E and I):
*   Intermediate nodes of Path 1: {B, C}
*   Intermediate nodes of Path 2: {M, L, J}
*   Intermediate nodes of Path 3: {F, K, G, H}

Comparing these sets, there are no common nodes among them. Therefore, these three paths are node-disjoint.

Since I have found three node-disjoint paths between E and I, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E from I is 3.

To confirm this, I need to identify a cut set of size 3. A valid cut set would consist of one intermediate node from each of these three disjoint paths. For example, I can choose the first intermediate node from each path:
*   From Path 1: B
*   From Path 2: M
*   From Path 3: F

Let's verify that removing the nodes [B, M, F] disconnects E from I:
1.  Removing B breaks Path 1 (E-B-C-I) and any other path starting E-B-....
2.  Removing M breaks Path 2 (E-M-L-J-I) and any other path starting E-M-....
3.  Removing F breaks Path 3 (E-F-K-G-H-I) and any other path starting E-F-....

Since E is only connected to B, M, and F, removing these three nodes will isolate E from the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between E and I.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, M, F]

3.  **Analyze Paths to F:**
    *   **Paths through E:** Node E is connected to M, L, N, and B. These connections provide various routes from J to E. For example, J -> M -> E -> F or J -> D -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> E -> F.
    *   **Paths through K or X:** Nodes K and X are both connected to F. They are also directly connected to each other (K-X). Crucially, both K and X are connected to node G. This means that any path from J to K or X (and subsequently to F) *must* pass through node G. For example, J -> ... -> H -> G -> K -> F or J -> ... -> H -> G -> X -> F.
4.  **Identify Critical Cut Points:** Based on the analysis above, all paths from J to F can be categorized into two independent sets:
    *   One set of paths goes through node E.
    *   The other set of paths goes through node G (which then leads to K or X, and finally to F).
5.  **Formulate a Cut Set:** To completely disconnect J from F, we must block all paths in both sets.
    *   Removing node E will sever all paths that go through E to F.
    *   Removing node G will sever all paths that go through G (and then to K or X) to F.
6.  **Verify Minimum Size:**
    *   If only E is removed, paths through G (e.g., J -> ... -> G -> K -> F) would still exist.
    *   If only G is removed, paths through E (e.g., J -> M -> E -> F) would still exist.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.
    *   Removing both E and G ensures that no path remains between J and F.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and a valid cut set is [E, G].

    *   Path 3: G - H - B - C - I

2.  **Paths starting E -> G:**
    *   From G, the only neighbors are F and K.
    *   G - F. F's neighbors are G, K, H. To reach I, F must connect to H.
        *   E - G - F - H - I (This path uses H)
        *   E - G - F - H - B - C - I (This path uses H)
        *   ... (any path from F to I must go through H or B/C/N/O/P/Y, which are all connected to H's sub-graph)
    *   G - K. K's neighbors are G, F, X.
        *   E - G - K - F - H - I (This path uses H)
        *   E - G - K - X - G - F - H - I (This path uses H)
    Upon careful inspection, all paths originating from E through G eventually lead to H to connect to I's side of the graph. Nodes F, K, X are only connected to each other or to G or H. None of them are connected to J, P, C (I's other neighbors). Therefore, all paths starting E -> G must pass through H.

3.  **Paths starting E -> M:**
    *   From M, the neighbors are E, L, J.
    *   M - J. J's neighbors are M, L, I, P, D.
        *   E - M - J - I (Direct path to I)
        *   E - M - J - P - I
        *   E - M - J - D - P - I
    *   M - L. L's neighbors are M, J.
        *   E - M - L - J - I (This path uses J)
        *   E - M - L - J - P - I (This path uses J)
        *   E - M - L - J - D - P - I (This path uses J)
    All paths originating from E through M must pass through J to connect to I.

**Summary of Path Analysis:**
Every path from E to I must pass through either node H (which covers all paths starting E->H and E->G) or node M (which covers all paths starting E->M, specifically through M->J).

**Identifying the Minimum Cut Set:**
*   If I remove node H, all paths that go through H (including those starting E->G) are broken. However, paths starting E->M (e.g., E - M - J - I) would still exist. So, removing H alone is not enough.
*   If I remove node M, all paths that go through M (e.g., E - M - J - I) are broken. However, paths starting E->H (e.g., E - H - I) would still exist. So, removing M alone is not enough.

Since all paths must pass through either H or M, removing both H and M will disconnect E and I.
Let's verify if any single node removal could disconnect E and I:
*   Removing any other node (G, F, K, X, B, C, N, O, Y, L, J, P, D) would leave at least one of the main "branches" (H or M) intact, allowing paths to persist. For example, removing J would break paths through M-J, but paths through H would remain.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2. The cut set consists of H and M.

Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M]

*   Removing {B, F}: Path E-I-H remains. (Not sufficient)

*   Removing {I, F}: Path E-B-H remains. (Not sufficient)
*   Removing {I, G}: Path E-B-H remains. (Not sufficient)
*   Removing {F, G}: Paths E-B-H and E-I-H remain. (Not sufficient)

    The edges connected to J are: (J, M), (J, I), (J, P), and (J, D).

    *   **Removing (J, I):** This breaks all paths that use I as the final connection to J. This includes E-H-I-J, E-H-B-C-I-J, E-G-F-H-I-J, and E-G-F-H-B-C-I-J.

4.  **Prove minimality (why 2 edges are not enough):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If I can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.

    Indeed, every path from N to G must include either H or F.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and removing two nodes (H and F) successfully disconnects N and G, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node B is connected to: N, C, E, H.
    *   Node H is connected to: B, G, I.

2.  **Analyze all possible paths between B and H:**
    Any path from B to H must eventually use one of the edges directly connected to H. These edges are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

    Let's list the general types of paths:
    *   **Direct path:** B - H (uses edge (B, H))
    *   **Paths via G:** B - ... - G - H (uses edge (G, H))
        *   Specifically, paths to G from B are: B - E - F - X - G and B - E - F - K - G.
    *   **Paths via I:** B - ... - I - H (uses edge (I, H))
        *   Paths to I from B are more numerous, for example:
            *   B - C - I
            *   B - C - P - I
            *   B - N - C - I
            *   B - N - C - P - I
            *   B - N - Y - O - P - I
            *   B - E - M - J - D - P - I
            *   B - E - L - M - J - D - P - I

3.  **Determine a potential cut set:**
    To disconnect B from H, all paths between them must be broken. Since all paths to H must pass through one of the edges (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H), removing these three edges will guarantee that H is completely isolated from B (and indeed, from the rest of the graph).
    This gives us a cut set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]. The size of this cut set is 3.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    Now, I need to prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges required. I will do this by showing that no set of 1 or 2 edges can disconnect B and H.

    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect B and H?**
        No. If I remove any single edge, at least two of the three critical edges ((B, H), (G, H), (I, H)) will remain, allowing a path. For example:
        *   Removing (B, H) leaves paths like B - C - I - H and B - E - F - X - G - H.
        *   Removing (G, H) leaves paths like B - H and B - C - I - H.
        *   Removing (I, H) leaves paths like B - H and B - E - F - X - G - H.
        *   Removing any other edge (e.g., (E, F)) would still leave all three critical edges intact, and thus multiple paths.

    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect B and H?**
        No. Consider any two edges {e1, e2}.
        *   If e1 and e2 are chosen from the set {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)}, then one of these three edges will always remain. For example, if I remove (B, H) and (G, H), the path B - C - I - H still exists via the edge (I, H). Similarly, removing (B, H) and (I, H) leaves (G, H) for paths like B - E - F - X - G - H. Removing (G, H) and (I, H) leaves (B, H) for the direct path B - H.
        *   If e1 or e2 (or both) are not from the set {(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)}, then at least two of these three critical edges will remain. For example, if I remove (E, F) and (C, I), the paths B - H, B - E - F - X - G - H (if (E,F) is not cut), and B - C - I - H (if (C,I) is not cut) would still exist. More importantly, the edges (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H) are all still present.

    Since removing any two edges leaves at least one path between B and H, a minimum of 3 edges must be removed.

5.  **Final Cut Set:**
    The set of edges [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)] successfully disconnects B from H, and it is the minimum possible size.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and E. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from E.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of H: B, C, I, G
    *   Neighbors of E: B, P, L, M

2.  **List all possible paths from H to E:**
    To ensure no path exists, we must consider all routes. I will list the main branches and then specific paths.

    *   **Paths through B:**
        *   H - B - E
        *   H - C - B - E
        *   H - C - N - B - E

    *   **Paths through P:**
        *   H - C - O - P - E
        *   H - I - P - E
        *   H - I - J - P - E
        *   H - G - F - P - E

    *   **Paths through M (and potentially L):**
        *   H - I - J - M - E
        *   H - I - J - M - L - E (Note: L is connected to M and E)

3.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. Let's find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible:

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - E
        (Edges: (H,B), (B,E))

    *   **Path 2:** H - C - O - P - E
        (Edges: (H,C), (C,O), (O,P), (P,E))
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - J - M - E
        (Edges: (H,I), (I,J), (J,M), (M,E))
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal breaks all paths between H and E. Based on the identified edge-disjoint paths, I will try to select one edge from each path that effectively cuts off a major branch leading to E.

    Consider the following edges:
    *   **(B,E):** This edge is the final link for all paths going through node B to E. Removing it breaks Path 1 (H-B-E) and all other paths through B (H-C-B-E, H-C-N-B-E).
    *   **(P,E):** This edge is the final link for all paths going through node P to E. Removing it breaks Path 2 (H-C-O-P-E) and all other paths through P (H-I-P-E, H-I-J-P-E, H-G-F-P-E).
    *   **(J,M):** This edge is crucial for paths going through J to M. Removing it breaks Path 3 (H-I-J-M-E). It also breaks the path H-I-J-M-L-E because M becomes disconnected from J (and thus from H). Since L is only connected to M and E, if M is disconnected from H, then L is also effectively disconnected from H.

5.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    Let's confirm that removing the edges **[(B,E), (P,E), (J,M)]** completely disconnects H from E.

    *   **Paths via B:** All paths to E that pass through B (e.g., H-B-E, H-C-B-E, H-C-N-B-E) are broken by removing (B,E).
    *   **Paths via P:** All paths to E that pass through P (e.g., H-C-O-P-E, H-I-P-E, H-I-J-P-E, H-G-F-P-E) are broken by removing (P,E).
    *   **Paths via M:** The only way to reach M from H is H-I-J-M. Removing (J,M) breaks this connection, so M is disconnected from H. Therefore, H cannot reach E via M.
    *   **Paths via L:** L is connected to M and E. Since M is disconnected from H (due to (J,M) being removed), H cannot reach L, and thus cannot reach E via L.

    All identified paths from H to E are broken by the removal of these 3 edges. Since we established that at least 3 edges are required, this set of 3 edges is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (P, E), (J, M)]

    *   E - F - H - ... (Similar to G, all paths from F to J must pass through H, as F is connected to G, H, K, X.)
    Therefore, any path starting E - F will eventually lead to H to reach J.

From this analysis, it becomes clear that all paths from E to J must pass through one of two critical nodes: M or H.
*   If a path uses M (e.g., E-M-J or E-L-M-J), removing M will break it.
*   If a path does not use M, it must use one of E's other neighbors (G, F, H, L).
    *   L is only connected to E and M. If M is removed, L cannot reach J.
    *   G and F are both connected to H. Any path from E through G or F to J must eventually pass through H.
    *   H is directly connected to E and leads to J's side of the graph.

Therefore, to disconnect E and J, we must remove both M and H.

Let's verify if a single node removal is sufficient:
*   If we remove M, paths like E-H-I-J still exist.
*   If we remove H, paths like E-M-J still exist.
*   If we remove any other single node (e.g., I, P, B, C, O, L, G, F, K, X, Y, D), there will always be at least one path remaining through M or H. For example, removing I still leaves E-M-J and E-H-B-C-O-P-J.

Since removing any single node is not sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2. The set {M, H} successfully disconnects E and J.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, H]

        *   E - M - J is broken.
        *   E - L - M - J is broken.

        *   E - F - K - J is broken.

    *   (J, D)
    *   (P, D)

This set of edges is [(J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D)]. The size of this cut set is 4. This confirms that 4 edges are sufficient to disconnect F and D.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut set exists (e.g., 3 edges):** To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to show that it's impossible to disconnect F and D by removing only 3 edges. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes (the edge-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If we can find 4 edge-disjoint paths from F to D, then the minimum cut size must be at least 4.

5.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from F to D:**
    Let's try to find four paths that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1:** F - M - J - D
        Edges: {(F, M), (M, J), (J, D)}

    *   **Path 2:** F - K - I - P - D
        Edges: {(F, K), (K, I), (I, P), (P, D)}

    *   **Path 3:** F - E - B - C - O - D
        Edges: {(F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, D)}

    *   **Path 4:** F - G - B - N - Y - D
        Edges: {(F, G), (G, B), (B, N), (N, Y), (Y, D)}

6.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    Let's list all edges from these four paths and check for duplicates:
    *   From Path 1: (F, M), (M, J), (J, D)
    *   From Path 2: (F, K), (K, I), (I, P), (P, D)
    *   From Path 3: (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, D)
    *   From Path 4: (F, G), (G, B), (B, N), (N, Y), (Y, D)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these four paths are distinct. For example:
    *   The initial edges from F (F, M), (F, K), (F, E), (F, G) are all different.
    *   The edges leading into D (J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D) are all different.

    Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between F and D, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect F and D is at least 4.

7.  **Conclusion:** We have found a cut set of 4 edges [(J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D)] that disconnects F and D, and we have proven that at least 4 edges are required. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer:

1.  Through the edge **(G, B)**, leading to node B (and then C, N, Y).
2.  Through the edge **(G, H)**, leading to node H (and then I, C).
3.  Through the edge **(F, E)**, leading to node E (and then B, L, M, J, P, I, O, N, Y, C).

**Verification of the proposed cut set:**
*   **Removing (G, B):** This severs all paths that go from X to G and then directly to B (e.g., X-G-B-C, X-F-G-B-C, X-K-G-B-C, and any paths through B-N-C or B-N-Y-C that use G-B).
*   **Removing (G, H):** This severs all paths that go from X to G and then to H (e.g., X-G-H-I-C, X-F-G-H-I-C, X-K-G-H-I-C).
*   **Removing (F, E):** This severs all paths that go from X to F and then to E. This covers a large number of paths, including those to B (X-F-E-B-C), to I (X-F-E-L-M-J-P-I-C), to O (X-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-C), and any subsequent paths through N or Y that rely on the F-E connection.

*   Any path starting X-F is either cut by (F,E) or, if it goes through G (X-F-G...), it's covered by the (G,B) or (G,H) cuts. If it goes through K then G (X-F-K-G...), it's also covered by (G,B) or (G,H) cuts.

1.  **Remove (G, B) and (G, H):**

    Therefore, this pair is not a cut set.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are I and E. My task is to find the minimum number of non-black nodes to remove to disconnect I from E.

I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between I and E. If I can find 'k' vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut) is at least 'k'.

Let's list paths from I to E:

1.  **Path 1: I - P - E**
    *   Intermediate node: P

2.  **Path 2: I - J - M - E**
    *   Intermediate nodes: J, M
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (P is not J or M).

3.  **Path 3: I - C - B - E**
    *   Intermediate nodes: C, B
    *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (P is not C or B) and Path 2 (J, M are not C or B).

These three paths (I-P-E, I-J-M-E, I-C-B-E) are vertex-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes. Therefore, to disconnect I from E, I must remove at least one node from each of these three paths. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects I and E.
Based on the three disjoint paths identified, a possible cut set would be to pick one node from each path:
*   From Path 1: P
*   From Path 2: J (or M)
*   From Path 3: C (or B)

Let's choose the cut set **[P, J, C]**. I will verify if removing these three nodes disconnects I from E.

1.  **Remove P**: This breaks Path 1 (I-P-E) and any other path that uses P, such as I-H-G-F-P-E, I-C-N-O-P-E, etc.
2.  **Remove J**: This breaks Path 2 (I-J-M-E) and any other path that uses J.
3.  **Remove C**: This breaks Path 3 (I-C-B-E) and any other path that uses C, such as I-H-C-B-E, I-C-N-O-P-E, etc.

After removing P, J, and C:
*   Node I is now only connected to H (since C, P, J are removed).
*   Node E is now connected to L, M, B (since P is removed).

Now, I need to check if there's any remaining path from I (via H) to E (via L, M, or B).
*   H is connected to G and I.
*   From G, we can go to F or K.
*   From F, we can go to G, X. (P is removed, so F cannot reach E via P).
*   From K, we can go to G, X.
*   From X, we can go to F, K.

The subgraph {H, G, F, K, X} is now isolated from the nodes L, M, B. There are no connections from any node in {H, G, F, K, X} to L, M, or B.
Therefore, removing nodes P, J, and C successfully disconnects I from E.

Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also found a cut set of size 3. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, J, C]

    *   **Category 2: Paths through F (and potentially K or X), then P, then to I (directly or via J).**
        Examples of such paths:
        *   G - F - P - I
        *   G - F - P - J - I
        *   G - K - F - P - I
        *   G - K - F - P - J - I
        *   G - X - F - P - I
        *   G - X - F - P - J - I

2.  **Identify critical edges that must be cut to sever these path categories.**
    *   To cut all paths in **Category 1 (through H)**, we must remove either the edge (G, H) or the edge (H, I).
    *   To cut all paths in **Category 2 (through F, then P)**, we need to find a bottleneck. All paths from G (via F, K, or X) that aim for I must eventually pass through node F, and from F, they must pass through the edge (F, P) to reach P. From P, they can reach I directly via (P, I) or indirectly via J (P-J-I). Therefore, removing the edge (F, P) will effectively cut off all paths in Category 2 from reaching P, and consequently I.

3.  **Formulate a minimal cut set.**
    Based on the analysis above, we need to remove at least one edge from Category 1 and at least one edge from Category 2.
    *   To cut Category 1, let's choose (G, H).
    *   To cut Category 2, let's choose (F, P).

    This gives us a cut set of two edges: [(G, H), (F, P)].

    *   If (F, P) is removed, all paths from G that go through F (or K or X, which lead to F) are blocked from reaching P. Since P is the only node connecting F to I (directly or via J), removing (F, P) ensures that G cannot reach I through this route.

    Since all identified paths from G to I are broken by removing these two edges, G and I are disconnected.

5.  **Confirm minimality.**

    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (F, P), then the path G-H-I still exists.
    *   Therefore, a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect G and I.
    *   Since we found a cut set of 2 edges that works, this is the minimum number of edges.

    *   F is connected to: P, G, K, X.
    *   J is connected to: P, I, M, D.

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (the min-cut), we can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between F and J (max-flow min-cut theorem).
    *   **Path 1:** F - P - J
        Edges: {(F, P), (P, J)}
    *   **Path 2:** F - G - H - I - J
        Edges: {(F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)}

    These two paths (Path 1 and Path 2) are edge-disjoint, meaning they do not share any common edges. Therefore, at least two edges must be removed to disconnect F from J. This establishes a lower bound for our answer: the minimum number of edges is at least 2.

3.  **Propose a cut set of size 2 and verify:**
    Based on the structure of the graph, let's consider cutting the "entry points" from F to the rest of the graph, or "chokepoints" that all paths must pass through.

    Consider the following cut set: **[(F, P), (G, H)]**.
    Let's verify if removing these two edges disconnects F from J:

    *   **Effect of removing (F, P):**
        Any path from F that starts with the edge (F, P) will be broken. This includes:
        *   F - P - J
        *   F - P - D - J
        *   F - P - E - M - J
        *   F - P - E - L - M - J
        All paths that use P as the first step from F are now disconnected.

    *   **Effect of removing (G, H):**
        Now, consider all paths from F that *do not* use (F, P). These paths must start from F via G, K, or X.
        *   If a path starts F - G: G is connected to F, K, X, H. To reach J, it must pass through H. If (G, H) is removed, F - G cannot reach H, and thus cannot reach J.
        *   If a path starts F - K: K is connected to F, G, X. To reach J, it must pass through G, then H. If (G, H) is removed, F - K - G cannot reach H, and thus cannot reach J.
        *   If a path starts F - X: X is connected to F, G, K. To reach J, it must pass through G, then H. If (G, H) is removed, F - X - G cannot reach H, and thus cannot reach J.

    Since all paths from F either start with (F, P) or eventually pass through (G, H) to reach the rest of the graph leading to J, removing both (F, P) and (G, H) will effectively disconnect F from J.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    The cut set [(F, P), (G, H)] consists of 2 edges. Since we determined that at least 2 edges are required, this cut set is minimal.

    *   Neighbors of E: {L, F, B, G}
    *   Neighbors of H: {G, I, B}

3.  **Explore paths between E and H:** To find the minimum cut, I will look for node-disjoint paths between E and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's find node-disjoint paths (paths that share no intermediate nodes):
    *   **Path 1: E - G - H**
        *   Intermediate node: G

    *   **Path 2: E - B - H**
        *   Intermediate node: B
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (G and B are distinct).

        *   Intermediate nodes: L, M, J, P, I
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (G is not in {L, M, J, P, I}) and Path 2 (B is not in {L, M, J, P, I}).

4.  **Identify a candidate cut set of size 3:**
    A common strategy for finding a cut set is to consider the direct neighbors of one of the target nodes. The neighbors of H are {G, I, B}. If all these nodes are removed, H will have no connections to the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from E.

    Let's test the set {G, I, B}:
    *   If G is removed, Path 1 (E-G-H) is broken.
    *   If B is removed, Path 2 (E-B-H) is broken.
    *   If I is removed, Path 3 (E-L-M-J-P-I-H) is broken.

    Since all three identified node-disjoint paths are broken by removing {G, I, B}, and any other path to H must necessarily pass through G, I, or B, removing these three nodes will indeed disconnect E from H.

3.  **Identify all distinct paths from H to N (or at least the "bottlenecks"):**

    Let's consider the "final" edges leading into N from the rest of the graph: (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N).

    *   **Paths through B to N:**
        *   H - B - N
        *   H - I - B - N
        *   H - E - B - N
        *   H - F - E - B - N
        *   H - G - F - E - B - N
        All these paths rely on the edge (B, N) as their final step to N.

    *   **Paths through C to N:**
        *   H - I - C - N
        *   H - E - I - C - N
        *   H - F - E - I - C - N
        *   H - G - F - E - I - C - N
        All these paths rely on the edge (C, N) as their final step to N.

    *   **Paths through O to N:**
        *   H - I - P - O - N
        *   H - E - I - P - O - N
        *   H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - F - E - I - P - O - N
        *   H - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   H - G - F - E - I - P - O - N
        *   H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        All these paths rely on the edge (O, N) as their final step to N.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut set:**
    *   Since all paths from H to N must pass through either B, C, or O, and then use the respective edge (B, N), (C, N), or (O, N) to reach N, we must remove at least one edge from each of these three distinct "entry points" to N.
    *   If we remove the edge (B, N), all paths from H that go through B to N are broken.
    *   If we remove the edge (C, N), all paths from H that go through C to N are broken.
    *   If we remove the edge (O, N), all paths from H that go through O to N are broken.
    *   By removing these three edges: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)], we ensure that N is completely isolated from any node that could be reached from H.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Could we disconnect H and N with only 2 edges?
    *   If we remove any two edges from the set {(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)}, the remaining edge would still allow paths to N. For example, if we remove (B, N) and (C, N), paths like H-I-P-O-N would still exist via (O, N).
    *   If we try to cut edges further away from N, for example, (H, B) and (I, C), many paths would still remain (e.g., H-E-B-N, H-I-P-O-N, H-E-I-P-O-N, etc.).
    *   Given the structure, the three edges (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N) represent three distinct "bottlenecks" right before N. To cut all paths, all three must be removed.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (O, N)]

    - (L, M) is unique to Path 3.

4.  **Identify edges directly connected to N:**
    *   (N, B)
    *   (N, C)
    *   (N, Y)

5.  **Propose a cut set:** Let's consider removing these three edges: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]. This set has a size of 3.

6.  **Verify the effectiveness of the proposed cut set:**
    *   Removing (N, B) ensures that no path can reach N via node B.
    *   Removing (N, C) ensures that no path can reach N via node C.
    *   Removing (N, Y) ensures that no path can reach N via node Y.
    *   Since B, C, and Y are the only nodes directly connected to N, removing these three edges completely isolates N from the rest of the graph. Therefore, no path can exist between G and N.

7.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If we were to remove only two of these edges (e.g., (N, B) and (N, C)), the edge (N, Y) would still remain. In this scenario, paths like G-E-L-M-J-P-O-Y-N would still connect G to N.
    *   Similarly, removing any other pair of these three edges would leave one connection to N intact, allowing paths to persist.
    *   Therefore, all three edges in the set [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)] are necessary to disconnect N from the graph, making this a minimal cut set.

8.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges that must be removed is 3.

The target nodes are N and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from H. This is a min-cut problem, and by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will identify the edges directly connected to the target nodes:
Node N is connected to: Y, O, B.
Node H is connected to: B, G, I.

Next, I will try to find edge-disjoint paths between N and H. An edge-disjoint path means that no two paths share any common edge.

Path 1: A direct path through B.
N - B - H
Edges: (N, B), (B, H)

Path 2: A path through O, C, and I.
N - O - C - I - H
Edges: (N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)
Let's check for edge-disjointness with Path 1:
- Edges from N: (N, B) in P1, (N, O) in P2. These are distinct.
- Edges to H: (B, H) in P1, (I, H) in P2. These are distinct.
- No other common edges between P1 and P2. So far, these two paths are edge-disjoint.

Path 3: A longer path through Y, O, P, J, M, L, E, F, X, G.
N - Y - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - X - G - H
Edges: (N, Y), (Y, O), (O, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)

- Edges from N: (N, B) in P1, (N, O) in P2, (N, Y) in P3. These are all distinct edges.
- Edges to H: (B, H) in P1, (I, H) in P2, (G, H) in P3. These are all distinct edges.

- No other common edges are found between P1, P2, and P3.

Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between N and H, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N and H is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that actually disconnects N and H.
A straightforward way to disconnect H from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
The edges connected to H are: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

After removing these three edges, node H has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between N and H.
Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H.

I will analyze all possible paths between E and H by categorizing them based on the initial edges from E and the final edges to H, and identifying critical bottlenecks.

**1. Direct Path:**
*   There is a direct edge (E, H). This path must be cut.

**2. Paths through the Left Cluster (F, G, K, X):**
*   Node E is connected to F and G.
*   Node H is connected to F and G.
*   Any path from E to H through this cluster must use an edge from E to {F, G} and an edge from {F, G} to H.
*   Examples: E-F-H, E-G-H, E-F-G-H, E-G-F-H, E-F-K-G-H, etc.
*   To cut all paths from E to H via this cluster, I can either:
    *   Remove all edges from E to this cluster: (E, F) and (E, G). (2 edges)
    *   Remove all edges from H to this cluster: (H, F) and (H, G). (2 edges)
    *   Remove a combination, e.g., (E, F) and (H, G). (2 edges)

**3. Paths through the Right Cluster (L, M, J, P, D, I, C, O, N, B, Y):**
*   Node E is connected to L and M.
*   Node H is connected to B and I.
*   Any path from E to H through this cluster must start with (E, L) or (E, M), and end with (B, H) or (I, H).
*   Let's trace the connections from E's side:
    *   E connects to L and M.
    *   L connects only to E and M.
    *   M connects to E, L, and J.
    *   This means any path from E into the deeper parts of the right cluster (J, P, D, I, C, O, N, B, Y) must pass through M, and then from M, it must pass through J.
    *   Therefore, the edge (M, J) is a critical bottleneck for all paths from E to H that go through the right cluster. If (M, J) is removed, E cannot reach J, P, D, I, C, O, N, B, Y, and thus cannot reach H via this cluster.

**Constructing a Minimal Cut Set:**

Based on the analysis, I need to cut paths from each of these three categories.

1.  **Cut the direct path:** Remove (E, H). (1 edge)
2.  **Cut paths through the left cluster:** To ensure E cannot reach H via F or G, I can remove (E, F) and (E, G). (2 edges)
3.  **Cut paths through the right cluster:** To ensure E cannot reach H via L/M to B/I, I can remove the bottleneck edge (M, J). (1 edge)

Combining these, a candidate cut set is: **[(E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (M, J)]**.
This set contains 1 + 2 + 1 = 4 edges.

**Verification of the Cut Set:**
*   Removing (E, H) breaks the direct connection.
*   Removing (E, F) and (E, G) ensures E cannot reach F or G. Since H's only connections to the left cluster are F and G, this effectively disconnects E from H through the left cluster.
*   Removing (M, J) ensures that E (via L or M) cannot reach J or any nodes beyond J (P, D, I, C, O, N, B, Y). Since H's connections to the right cluster (B, I) are only reachable through this part of the graph, this effectively disconnects E from H through the right cluster.
All paths between E and H are broken.

**Minimality Check (using Max-Flow Min-Cut Theorem):**
To prove this is the minimum, I need to find 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

1.  **Path 1:** E - H (Edges: (E, H))
2.  **Path 2:** E - F - H (Edges: (E, F), (F, H))
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.
3.  **Path 3:** E - G - H (Edges: (E, G), (G, H))
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (as F and G are distinct nodes, and their connections to E and H are distinct).
4.  **Path 4:** E - L - M - J - I - H (Edges: (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H))
    *   Let's check for edge-disjointness with Paths 1, 2, and 3.
        *   (E, L) is distinct from (E, H), (E, F), (E, G).
        *   (I, H) is distinct from (E, H), (F, H), (G, H).
        *   The intermediate edges (L, M), (M, J), (J, I) are new and not used in Paths 1, 2, or 3.
    *   Therefore, Path 4 is edge-disjoint from Paths 1, 2, and 3.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 4. As I have found a cut set of size 4, this is indeed the minimum.

Alternative cut sets of size 4 exist, for example, by choosing (H,F) and (H,G) instead of (E,F) and (E,G). For instance, [(E, H), (H, F), (H, G), (M, J)] is also a valid minimal cut set.

3.  **Search for Node-Disjoint Paths**: To find the minimum number of nodes to remove (minimum vertex cut), I will look for node-disjoint paths between E and J. The number of node-disjoint paths gives a lower bound for the minimum cut size (Menger's Theorem).

    *   **Path 1**: E - M - J
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding E and J): {M}

    *   **Path 2**: E - I - J
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding E and J): {I}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1, as {M} and {I} are distinct.

    *   **Path 3**: Now I need to find a path that does not use M or I.
        *   Let's try starting from E, avoiding M and I: E - H - ...
        *   From H, avoiding M and I: H - B - ... (H also connects to G, but G is further away from J's side)
        *   From B, avoiding M and I: B - N - ... (B also connects to C, but C connects to I, which is removed. B-C-O-P-J is also a path, but B-N-O-P-J is also valid)
        *   From N, avoiding M and I: N - O - ... (N also connects to C, but C connects to I. N-C-O-P-J is also a path)
        *   From O, avoiding M and I: O - P - ...
        *   From P, avoiding M and I: P - J
        *   So, **Path 3**: E - H - B - N - O - P - J
        *   Nodes in this path (excluding E and J): {H, B, N, O, P}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 ({M}) and Path 2 ({I}).

4.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size**: Since I have found three node-disjoint paths (E-M-J, E-I-J, and E-H-B-N-O-P-J), the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and J must be at least 3.

5.  **Identify a Candidate Cut Set of Size 3**: Based on the paths identified, a natural candidate for a cut set would be {M, I, P}. These nodes are critical points in the paths.

6.  **Verify the Candidate Cut Set {M, I, P}**: I will now check if removing nodes M, I, and P successfully disconnects E from J.

    *   **Paths through M**: Any path using M (e.g., E-M-J, E-L-M-J) is broken because M is removed.
    *   **Paths through I**: Any path using I (e.g., E-I-J, E-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J) is broken because I is removed.
    *   **Paths through P**: Any path using P (e.g., E-...-P-J) is broken because P is removed.

    Let's consider any remaining paths from E to J after removing M, I, and P:
    *   The only remaining neighbor of J is D. However, D is only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D.
    *   From E, the remaining neighbors are F, L, H.
        *   L is only connected to M, which is removed. So E-L is a dead end.
        *   F connects to K, X, G.
        *   H connects to B, G.
        *   From F, G, H, B, C, N, O, K, X, Y, there is no way to reach J because M, I, and P (the critical connections to J) have been removed, and D is isolated from E's side.

    Therefore, removing {M, I, P} successfully disconnects E and J.

7.  **Conclusion**: Since the minimum cut size is at least 3 (from node-disjoint paths) and I have found a cut set of size 3 ({M, I, P}), this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]

    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect F and J. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

        *   As established in step 4, all paths from {G, K, X} to the rest of the graph (excluding F) must pass through node H.
        *   Therefore, if I remove H, all paths from F (via G, K, X) will be severed from the rest of the graph, including J.
    *   **Conclusion:** Removing both P and H will disconnect F and J.
        *   Removing P breaks F-P-J and all paths that use P.
        *   Removing H breaks all paths from F (via G, K, X) to the rest of the graph (I, C, B, E, M, L, J).
        *   Thus, F and J are completely disconnected.

    *   Path 7: B - C - N - O - P - J - M
    *   Path 8: B - C - O - P - J - M

    *   Removing C: Paths 5, 6, 7, 8 are broken. Paths 1, 2, 3, 4, 9 (e.g., B-H-E-M) remain.
    *   Removing N: Paths 7, 9 are broken. Paths 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8 (e.g., B-H-E-M) remain.

    *   Removing L: Path 2 is broken. Paths 1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 (e.g., B-H-E-M) remain.

    *   Removing P: Paths 4, 6, 7, 8, 9 are broken. Paths 1, 2, 3, 5 (e.g., B-H-E-M) remain.
    *   Removing O: Paths 7, 8, 9 are broken. Paths 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 (e.g., B-H-E-M) remain.
    Since no single node can disconnect B and M, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Find a Minimum Cut Set of Size 2:**
    I observe that all paths from B to M can be categorized into two groups based on the nodes they pass through just before reaching M's immediate vicinity:
    *   Group A: Paths that pass through node E (Paths 1 and 2).
    *   Group B: Paths that pass through node J (Paths 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, and 9).

    *   After removing E, all remaining paths (Paths 3-9) still connect B to M, and they all pass through J.

        *   Edges: (H, B), (B, E)
